# Changelog

All notable changes to Agent Farm. Detailed release notes live in the built-in guide (`Agent Farm: Open Guide` → Release Notes / Sürüm Notları).

## 3.137.0 — Six of the eight bridge-parity items: the farm can now be set up, measured and tidied by instruction (2026-08-15)

The rule in `_REF/agentfarm-dev.md` says that anything a person can trigger from a panel or the palette, and that an agent could reasonably be told to do, reaches the bridge in the same commit. B-3 is the programme that goes back and settles where that was not done. Six of its items ship here — **62 new operations** — and every one of them calls the same core function the page calls, because a bridge that grows a second implementation is a bridge that disagrees with the surface it copies.

**B-4 — a run's whole life, not just its beginning.** Starting a task, finishing one by hand, releasing one a ceiling is holding, moving it in the queue, undoing a delete, picking a cut chain back up, running a finished mission again, dropping one from the history: eight gestures that existed only under a pointer.

- **‼️ `tasks.start` is not `tasks.runNext`.** One starts the task where it stands; the other bumps it over every other queued task first. They were one op, which meant "start this" silently reordered the queue. And if a ceiling holds the launch, the answer is `started:false` with `heldBy` naming it — a caller told "started" about a run that never launched is the bug this exists to avoid.
- **‼️ `tasks.unpark` ends the park EPISODE.** Releasing a held run and having the same ceiling catch it again is news a second time, not a silent second hold. The release itself moved into `TaskManager.unparkTask`, so the row's "retry start" button and the op are one implementation.
- `tasks.move` takes an exact place in the queue (`to: 0` is next up), not just one step at a time — repeating a one-step move is how a queue ends up half sorted. `tasks.restore` brings a deleted run back from the month archive and says plainly that its **output file is not coming back**.
- `missions.rerun` is cost-bearing and says so; `missions.run.delete` reports `specDeleted`, because "that run is gone" and "the mission is gone from the list" are different answers.

**B-5 — findings, suggestions, quality and eval cases.** `insights.list` now carries each finding's **`key`**: a finding is re-derived on every scan, so its wording was never a handle. `insights.dismiss` hides one for a stated time — `days:0` is an error rather than quietly meaning forever — `insights.restore` un-hides, and `insights.delegate` folds several findings into **one** run, because five agents editing one file is not a fix. Instruction suggestions get list/scan/apply/dismiss, and `evals.delete` finally exists. The three that run a model refuse without `confirm:true`.

**B-7 — the settings page, by instruction.** Twenty operations over Agent Farm's settings, the saved setting sets, Claude Code's own `settings.json`, the agent roots and the per-agent engine defaults.

- **‼️ The SCHEMA is the allowlist** — the same model the page draws itself from, so a setting added tomorrow is writable with no extra code and an undeclared key is refused. Values go through the page's own validation.
- **‼️ A credential-shaped value never comes back.** A key whose name ends in Key / Token / Password is masked; a key that holds a secret's *name* is not. The Secrets vault stays off the bridge.
- **‼️ A setting set is a complete state, not an overlay** — applying one also resets every modified key it does not mention, and `dryRun` names every step before anything moves. On the Claude side there is deliberately **no raw-overwrite op**: only the catalogued lines, and a `settings.json` that is not valid JSON is refused rather than clobbered.

**B-8 — the working infrastructure.** The live hooks (user and agent scope), the headless Windows service, the usage lap and the price of a model nobody priced.

- **‼️ Anything that changes the machine PLANS first.** `service.install` called bare returns the exact scheduled-task name, every file it would write and the current state, and changes nothing; only `confirm:true` acts. `schtasks` is now called with an **argument vector rather than a command line**, so no path with a space or a quote in it can become a second command, and the result is verified by reading the task back.
- **‼️ `pricing.rules` names the models this farm has really run that match no rule** — they are billed at $0, so a real spend reads as free and every cost figure above it is quietly wrong. `pricing.set` upserts one rule, compiles the regex before saving, and refuses a negative rate or an empty table.

**B-9 — every document the extension produces.** The cost report opened an editor, the digest opened a save dialog, the OTLP export asked where to put it — so an agent told "work out why yesterday was expensive and write it up" could not reach a single one of them.

- **One delivery contract:** the op returns the **content**. Pass `file` and it is written there through a temp-and-rename, and the answer **always names the path**, including when a default was chosen for you.
- `sessions.context` captures a closed session's real per-category token table by running Claude's own local `/context` — it spends no tokens. Two refusals are deliberate: a **live** session is never touched (a second writer on one transcript), and a non-Claude engine is told so rather than guessed at.
- `digest.daily` only *shows* the digest; `send` and `speak` are explicit. `reports.weekly` runs a model and refuses without `confirm:true`.

**B-10 — a file the farm did not generate.** `studio.asset.register` files a hand-downloaded render or a clip made in another tool into the gallery. Every path is judged **before** a single sidecar is written, so a bad one in the middle registers nothing; a file already registered comes back in `alreadyKnown` rather than as an error.

**Still open in B-3:** B-6 (memory maintenance and semantic operations) and B-11 (the permanent parity gate, which by design runs last). Tests: 157 files, 9577 assertions.

## 3.136.0 — The Console reads where you left it, what you left it on, and as far back as you like (2026-08-14)

Four backlog items, all of them about the same complaint from different sides: the conversation panel losing track of the reader.

**K-19 — the new-line badge and the reading position, on the rare turn where they went.** Reported as *occasional*, and that is exactly what the cause looks like. `unseen` was counted as the growth in the number of ROWS, which is right for almost everything the Console draws — a tool call, its result, the closing figures — and wrong for the one case where it matters most: a long answer streams into the SAME message object, so the row count never moves and the badge stayed hidden while paragraphs piled up below the fold. That case is now measured as well as counted, and only when the row count did not move, so the ordinary path is untouched down to the arithmetic.

- **‼️ …and the reading position is now GUARANTEED rather than assumed.** `talkinner` is rewritten whole on every repaint, and leaving `scrollTop` alone preserves the view only while the pixels above the fold stay the same pixels — true almost always, which is why this almost always worked. Every row now publishes its message id (`data-mid`), so the row at the top of the viewport is found again after the write and put back on the same pixel. **In the ordinary repaint the drift is zero and no scroll is written at all**: it is a guarantee laid over a rule that was already right, not a replacement for it.
- `test/test_console_tail.js` drives the real page against a real viewport — row heights, a clamped `scrollTop`, an anchor measured in pixels — because every string assertion in this repo passes just as happily on a page that has thrown the reader two hundred lines up.

**K-21 — `show` belongs to the card, not to the panel.** Two sessions side by side are two different readings: one you are following line by line, one you only want the dialogue of. The filter was a single panel-wide value, so pressing a card silently re-filtered it to whatever the last card had been left on — and the lines that vanished looked like lines that had never arrived. Each session now keeps its own; a card that has never been filtered starts from the panel's standing default, and a card taken off the shelf takes its filter with it.

**K-23 — the part of the conversation above the scrollback ceiling is readable from the panel.** A resume rebuilds the last 150 rows (600 at the outside) so a forty-megabyte transcript cannot stall the panel. Everything older was in the file and nowhere else — the Console had had the conversation and was the one surface that would not show it. The head row now carries **`↑ load 200 older`**, and pressing it walks up the thread a chunk at a time.

- **‼️ It cannot draw the same turn twice, by construction.** Every scrollback row is named by its POSITION in the transcript, so two reads of an append-only file agree on every row below the tail and an overlap is an id collision to skip. Each chunk states which reading of the file it came from.
- **‼️ And it does not move you.** The boundary marker is excluded from the reading anchor above, so the chunk lands behind it and the line you were on stays under your eye.
- **The one case it refuses.** Only the tail of a huge transcript is parsed, so there a row's position is its place in *that* tail and the next read would number it differently — the handle is not offered, and the sentence stands as it did. Reading costs nothing either way: the file is on this disk and no token is spent.
- On the bridge: **`console.older`** — same read, answering `{ added, older, snapshot }`.

**U-52 (closing) — the named subsets, on the one control that was still spelt out a press at a time.** The sweep in 3.122.3 gave every multi-pick box in the extension the subsets its list has a word for. Missions' status row is the same question in a different control: seven chips that nobody reads as seven. It now carries **Waiting on you · Still going · Finished · Clear** beside them — and "which of these need me?" is one press instead of two out of seven, with knowing which two.

## 3.135.1 — K-22, measured against the CLI itself rather than its documentation (2026-08-14)

The process smoke for 3.135.0 was run against the installed CLI, and reading the binary to explain its answer corrected two things in the release that had just shipped.

- **‼️ The policy key was being read at the wrong path, and in the wrong place.** The CLI's own resolution is `n.permissions?.disableBypassPermissionsMode === "disable"` over the **merged** settings — so it is nested under `permissions`, not a top-level key, and it counts from whichever layer states it, not only the managed file. 3.135.0 checked a top-level key in the managed file alone: a policy the CLI obeys would have passed straight through it, which is the exact shape of the bug this item is about.
- **`--allow-dangerously-skip-permissions` turns out to be enough for a settings bypass** — `if (s || t.allowDangerouslySkipPermissions) f.push(v)` — which is why the smoke's negative-control lane could not reproduce the old behaviour on a machine whose `~/.claude/settings.json` already says `permissions.defaultMode: "bypassPermissions"`. The control now says that in words and skips, rather than reporting a pass it did not earn. It changes nothing about the fix: `--dangerously-skip-permissions` short-circuits at the top of that function and does not depend on the settings branch resolving at all.
- **`test/smoke_bypass.mjs`** — the process smoke, on the installed CLI, running the exact recipe from the report (a Git Bash compound with a variable assignment, a redirect into `%APPDATA%\…\bridge` outside the working directory, then wait/read/remove). **Measured: `can_use_tool` 0, one tool call, on the argv the Console now builds.** haiku, one turn, per the test-model policy.
- One thing no file on this machine can answer, so it is written down instead: the CLI can also disable bypass from a remote feature gate (`tengu_disable_bypass_permissions_mode`). If that fires, the process starts in `default` behind a bypass label — and the auto-answer above is what keeps the contract anyway.

## 3.135.0 — "Bypass" on the card now means bypass in the process (2026-08-14)

**K-22 — a session labelled bypassPermissions that then stopped and asked.** The user's decision on this is a contract, not a preference: bypass means *whatever happens, do not ask me*. It was not being kept, and the cause was one flag standing in for another. S-18 (3.93.0) added `--allow-dangerously-skip-permissions` to the hosted launch — a flag whose own help says it "enables bypassing all permission checks **as an option**, without it being enabled by default". It permits bypass to be chosen later; it does not apply it. Measured on the reported session (`1963cc5a`, Claude 2.1.232): settings, the first native user event and the Console state all said `bypassPermissions`, while the process argv held only `--permission-prompt-tool stdio --allow-dangerously-skip-permissions`, and ordinary Bridge Bash calls went on producing `can_use_tool`.

- **‼️ The permission mode is RESOLVED before the spawn, not assumed from the picker.** Three facts are now separate, because collapsing them is what broke the contract: what was **requested** (the picker, `''` = Default), what Claude's own settings layers make Default **mean** (managed → project-local → project → user, in the CLI's own precedence), and what the launch actually **enforced**. An explicit bypass, or a Default that resolves to bypass through `permissions.defaultMode`, is launched with **`--dangerously-skip-permissions`** — the flag that applies it — and with no `--permission-mode` beside it. Every other mode's command line is byte-for-byte what it was.
- **‼️ Bypass that cannot be enforced does not start a session.** A managed policy (`disableBypassPermissionsMode: "disable"`, honoured only in the managed file, as Claude honours it) means the session appears **in error**, naming the policy and its file, rather than being quietly downgraded to asking. A card that says bypass over a process that asks is the one outcome this refuses to produce.
- **‼️ And if a permission request arrives anyway, the user never sees it.** A bypassed process is not supposed to send `can_use_tool` at all, so one arriving is a CLI regression — and the decision covers the regression too. It is answered automatically, counted (`bypassAutoApproved`, visible on the card and on the bridge, because a number above zero is evidence), and no window is opened. **Two things are deliberately excluded.** A question for the *person* (AskUserQuestion) is not a permission prompt and still reaches them. And the one shape bypass refuses instead of approving: a recursive force delete aimed at `/`, `~`, `$HOME` or a drive root, a disk format, a raw device overwrite — denied automatically, said out loud, and handed back to the agent as an ordinary tool error. "Never ask me" is not consent to that.
- **The surfaces say the same thing.** The card's tooltip leads with the mode **in force** and where it came from ("Default resolves to bypassPermissions through …"), not the empty picker value; the permissions box mirrors it. On the bridge, `console.sessions` carries `enforcedPermissionMode`, `permissionWhy` and `bypassAutoApproved` beside the requested mode.

> **Not yet verified live.** The acceptance for this item requires a smoke test on the installed VSIX across Claude 2.1.226 / .229 / .232 — explicit bypass, Default→bypass, a saved profile, a live switch, a resume, and the real AppData Bridge recipe, asserting **zero** permission dialogs. The 48 checks in `test/test_bypass_contract.js` drive the real resolver, the real argument builder, the real reducer and the real `ConsoleHost`; they are not a substitute for that run.

## 3.134.0 — A transcript too large to read is not a session that never happened (2026-08-14)

**S-32 — the 24 MB silent limit that dropped a whole session out of the index.** Found while measuring S-30, and it was one long conversation away from happening for real. `readCapped` in the external-transcript scan returned `undefined` for any file over 24 MB, and every parser reads `undefined` as *not a session*: the row left the external list entirely and took the Sessions table, the cost totals, the project match, Console's resume scrollback and search with it — **and no surface anywhere said why**. The reported Codex rollout measured **13 970 211 bytes, 58% of that ceiling**; the same session an hour longer would simply have vanished.

- **‼️ A JSONL rollout is streamed now, so its size decides nothing.** The scan reads it a megabyte at a time and never holds the file: the guard is 512 MB and it exists for a file that is no longer a transcript, not for a long conversation. A 26 MB fixture is now read whole and its figures are real — turns, tools, tokens, cost, ctx. The chunk boundary is decoded properly, so a Turkish character that straddles two reads is not torn into a replacement character in somebody's prompt (checked with 400 prompts of `ışığın önündeki…` across the boundary).
- **‼️ A format that must be held whole is LISTED unread, never dropped.** A gemini chat file and the Console's own snapshot are one JSON document each, so those keep a ceiling — and above it the session is still on the table: real id, real file, real time, every figure an honest zero, and an **`unread` badge** on the row whose hover carries the two figures that decided it (`transcript 30.0 MB · scan limit 24.0 MB`). The Tokens and Cost hovers open with `Not read: …` so a zero that was never measured cannot be mistaken for a session that did nothing. Cost totals are untouched — nothing is invented.
- On the bridge: `scanLimit` on `sessions.find` and `sessions.recent` rows, for the same reason — an agent reading those zeros had no way to tell them from a quiet session.

**S-30 (closing) — the snapshot is taken when the file has stopped moving, and it says which snapshot it is.** 3.133.0 stopped a resume from trading the conversation on screen for what disk gave back. This is the other half: the read itself.

- **‼️ Stat, read, stat again.** The scrollback read was a single unlocked glance at a file the engine had finished writing seconds earlier; landing mid-flush returns a file a few lines short, and the missing lines are the closing turn — the one the reader came back for. Equal size and mtime either side of the read means nothing moved under it. If it moved, the read is repeated up to three times; a file still moving after that is **reported as still being written** — "Resume is ready either way, the engine is resumed by its id" — instead of being drawn as a conversation with a hole in it.
- **‼️ The scrollback names its snapshot.** The closing line now reads `— read back from the transcript · snapshot 13.97 MB @ 14/08/2026 12:04:33 · resumed here —`, so what the panel is showing can be held against the file "Open transcript" opens rather than taken on trust.
- A transcript past the read ceiling gets a sentence saying so, with the size — the S-32 rule applied on the reading side, where an empty panel used to be the only answer.

## 3.133.0 — A resume never trades the conversation you can see for what a file happens to give back (2026-08-14)

**S-30 — "End Session, then Resume, and the conversation I had just had was gone."** Reported once, on a 13-turn Codex session. It was measured before anything was changed, and the finding is that nothing was lost: the rollout on disk is complete (13 970 211 bytes, 2 466 lines, **0 unparseable**), the parser reads **1 127 messages** out of it in 90 ms, and the closing user prompt and agent answer are both in the last 150 of them. The writing side was never the fault. The reading-back side had **three ways to show an empty conversation and no way to say why**, and all three are now closed.

- **‼️ What the card is already showing is never traded for what disk says.** This is the fault the report is about. Resuming an ended session reuses its card (S-29) — and the old code *deleted that card's record* and re-seeded it with whatever the transcript read returned, **including nothing**. The messages on the card were the ground truth for everything the reader had just watched happen live. Now the richer of the two wins: a read that comes back empty, short, or as a one-line "could not read it" note leaves the conversation on the card untouched. They are never merged — the same turn read twice from two sources is a duplicated conversation, which is the other half of this bug and it is not being traded for the first.
- **‼️ A blank scrollback is never silent again.** Every failure on that path returned `[]`, which the panel draws exactly like a session where nothing was ever said. Each of the three now says which one happened, because they need different answers from the reader: the index does not know this id yet · the file could not be read (with the reason) · it was read and held no conversation. In every case the line also says the part that matters — the engine is resumed by its id, so its own context is intact.
- **‼️ The last completed turn is not subject to the row budget.** The backfill was `slice(-150)`, and on a tool-heavy engine 150 rows is one turn's tool traffic: measured on the reported session, the last 150 messages span **2 turns out of 13**, so a slightly busier turn would have swallowed both the question and the answer — from a transcript that has them. The budget is a floor now: the window starts at whichever is earlier, the last 150 messages or the opening line of the turn in progress, capped at 600 so a huge turn cannot ask the panel to draw the file. A turn past that cap carries its prompt in at the front on its own — *what did I ask* is the half of an exchange nobody can reconstruct from the rest. **Nothing changes when the last turn already fits**, which is the ordinary case and was the case in the report.
- Every engine, both console shapes: the long-lived Claude process and the per-turn engines (Codex, Gemini, Antigravity), which is the side the report came from.

## 3.132.0 — "Nothing was attempted" is now a finding, and a retry can pick work up instead of starting it again (2026-08-14)

The two halves of the same 45-minute run. It worked, wrote 269 tool calls into its transcript, met the session limit on the way out — and was reported as a run where *nothing was attempted*, after which six retries resent the original prompt into six fresh conversations that could not see any of that work. **M-12** is the diagnosis; **M-10's second half** is the treatment. S-31 (3.131.0) is what made both possible: until a run was bound to its own transcript there was no evidence to reason from.

**M-12 — a `failed` badge is not one answer.** `retryDecision` produced the sentence "the session limit was hit, so nothing was attempted" the instant the limit regex matched — without looking at elapsed time, at the transcript, at assistant or tool activity, or at anything else. For the failure it was written about (the CLI refusing a run outright) that is true; for a run cut off mid-flight it is the opposite of the truth. And `claude -p` prints the RESULT rather than the working, so when the limit arrives before the final message the output file holds the limit sentence and nothing else — the output can never be the evidence.

- **Three phases, and only one may say "nothing was attempted".** `not-started` — the engine began no turn. `interrupted` — the run's own transcript shows assistant text, tool use, tool results. `unknown` — nothing is bound to this run yet, which is a real state and is written down as one rather than being quietly rounded down to "nothing happened".
- **‼️ Time is not progress.** A run can hold a lock for an hour and attempt nothing, so elapsed time never promotes a phase on its own. Assistant messages and tool calls do.
- **The classification improves itself.** A failure is first recorded as `unknown` — honest, because the transcript is usually indexed a beat later — and upgraded the moment S-31's binding lands, which may be minutes or a restart afterwards. The upgrade is written to the run's own event log, so the correction is visible rather than silent.
- **The row says it.** Under the `failed` badge: `45 min of work · 269 tool call(s) · the run was CUT OFF, not untouched`. In the tooltip, the warning that follows from it: read the transcript before sending the same work again. On the bridge: `failurePhase`, `failureSummary`, `progress`, `runSession` and `continuationOf` on `tasks.get`, and `failurePhase`/`didWork` on `tasks.list` — because an agent polling for `failed` had no way to tell the two apart either, and resending the work is right for one and destructive for the other.

**M-10 (the half about WHAT) — a retry is a fresh run, or a continuation.** The policy's first half decided WHEN a retry goes. This decides what it sends, and the two are independent: a run can wait perfectly for the window and still throw away forty-five minutes by resending the original prompt into a conversation that cannot remember them.

- **`simple`** — a retry is a fresh run with the original prompt (or the retry prompt). **This is the default and nothing migrates:** a policy written before this field behaves exactly as it did, or every scheduled run in the fleet would change meaning on an upgrade.
- **`smart`** — a retry after a run that had ALREADY DONE WORK is a continuation. It waits for the window, **resumes that run's own session** (S-19's machinery, one resume builder for all four engines) and sends the **continuation prompt** as the next turn. Which of the two happens is decided by M-12's phase, never guessed.
- **‼️ `unknown` is HELD, not repeated.** The retry is created and visibly parked with `progress:unknown` as its blocker — and when the evidence lands it is not merely unparked, it is REMADE into the right shape: a continuation if work was cut off, an ordinary retry if none had started. `park` is the default; `simple`, `stop` and `ask` are the other answers, and `ask` is the user's own hold — evidence arriving answers the machine's question, not theirs, so it does not release the row behind their back.
- **The continuation words are yours.** 1st, 2nd and 3rd-and-after pick-ups can differ; empty uses the built-in default, which tells the engine to READ the transcript and the working folder before writing and not to redo finished work. Placeholders `{taskTitle} {attempt} {failureReason} {resetAt} {progressSummary}` — an unknown one is left in the text rather than silently blanked, because a typo that deletes half a sentence is worse than one you can see in the prompt the run is about to be given.
- **The row reads as one job.** A continuation is titled `(continue 3)` rather than `(retry 3)`, carries `continuationOf`, and resumes the conversation it follows — so seven attempts of one piece of work stop looking like seven unrelated failures. And smart continuation covers only the attempts of ONE logical run: a schedule's next ordinary firing never inherits a conversation.
- **A continuation needs a session to continue.** Without one there is nothing to resume, and opening a NEW conversation with a prompt that says "carry on from where you stopped" would be the worst of both — so it falls back to an ordinary retry and says so on the record.

## 3.131.0 — A run the farm started is never a stranger to it (2026-08-14)

**S-31 — the 45-minute run that was not on the board.** A scheduled task ran for 45 minutes, wrote a 3.2 MB transcript with 269 tool calls, and was in no list Agent Farm draws: not in `sessions.find`, not under Sessions → Auto, not in the Console's Resume list, not on its agent's page. Its six limit-retries were missing the same way. The data was never lost — the extension simply could not find its own work.

**Why.** The index decides whose session a transcript is by asking where it ran: a cwd under an agent's folder gets that agent, anything else gets nothing — and with `includeUnmatchedProjects` off (the default) a session with no agent is DROPPED. That is right for a stranger's conversation and wrong for a run the farm spawned itself in a code repo. And it could not be repaired downstream, because the repair needed the thing that was missing: the launcher map is fed from a task's matched transcript, the match runs over that agent's sessions, and a dropped session is in nobody's list. Binding needed a match; matching needed a binding.

- **A launch binding is written for every headless run** — task, schedule, trigger, workflow, mission — out of what the launcher already knows: agent, project, mechanism, engine, **the folder it was actually spawned in**, and its lifespan. It is built from the task store, which is already persisted and already rewritten on every change, so a restart rebuilds every binding from the same evidence and there is no second file to fall out of step.
- **‼️ It is consulted BEFORE the scope filter, not after it.** That is the whole fix: the filter's question is "is this one of ours", and a run that said so at the spawn has already answered it. The user's own "other projects" setting is untouched — it still decides what happens to conversations the farm did NOT start.
- **Three proofs, in order of certainty.** `declared` — the id was known before the process started (a resumed conversation, S-19). `reported` — the transcript was named by the cost matcher. `matched` — engine, folder and lifespan named exactly one. Two of our own runs overlapping in the same folder on the same engine produce an **ambiguity, never a pick**: filing a real transcript under the wrong run is worse than the gap, because a gap is visible.
- **A binding never overrules the path.** A transcript genuinely inside an agent's folder belongs to that agent; a run record cannot move it. What the binding always supplies is the launcher, because "which of our mechanisms started this" is a fact no path can tell.
- **A retry inherits the launcher of the run it is a retry of.** `fromSchedule` does not travel down the `retryOf` chain, so six limit-retries of one scheduled run each read as something a person had typed. The chain is walked now (with a hop limit, so a hand-edited store cannot loop it) and all seven rows carry the schedule's name and read as Auto.
- **The answer travels back to the run record.** The cost path only runs for fifteen minutes after a run ends, so a session found later — on the next start-up, or when a transcript is indexed after the fact — left the Tasks row still saying there was nothing to open. The transcript link is now written on its own, idempotently, without inventing a cost.
- **The dropped files are remembered**, as the five fields a binding needs to test one. So a binding that arrives after the scan (which is the ordinary case: the transcript is written and indexed *while* the run goes) rescues the file without re-walking every projects folder on the machine.
- **The backfill is the same mechanism, not a migration.** Bindings are published before the first scan and built from every run in the store, so the runs that were already lost come back on the next start. Measured on this machine: **the session and its six retries are indexed, owned by `developer`, and carry the schedule that started them.** An archived run is the one case that does not come back — the archive's reader carried no working folder or engine, and binding it would be the silent guess this item exists to remove.
- On the bridge: **`sessions.bindings`** — which run produced which transcript, what proof, and the transcripts that more than one run could have written. The question to ask when a run you know happened is not on the board.

## 3.130.0 — A task can continue a conversation, and a retry can wait for the window rather than guess at it (2026-08-14)

The run that wrote most of this hit the session limit halfway through and its own six retries — forty minutes apart, blind — ran out seven minutes before the window reopened. That is the shape of the two items below: one is about WHICH conversation a run happens in, the other about WHEN a failed one is tried again.

**S-19 — a task can be the next turn of a conversation instead of a new one.** "Carry on with what you were doing" used to mean pasting the last output into a fresh prompt, which is a copy of the context and not the context. The New-task form now has a **Conversation** picker: *a new session*, or *continue an existing conversation…* — and the second opens a searchable list of the conversations that genuinely can be resumed, narrowed by the farm's own query grammar over name, agent, project, engine and id. The engine really resumes it, so the transcript and everything it had read carry on in place, while the run stays its own task row, its own events and its own archived output.

- **Each engine's own vocabulary, from one place.** `--resume <id>` before `-p` on Claude; `codex exec resume <thread>` as a SUBCOMMAND, without the arguments resume refuses and with the sandbox moved onto `-c`; Gemini's own `--resume`; and Antigravity, which calls it a `--conversation`. One builder, so a run and the Console cannot drift apart about what resuming means.
- **An engine that cannot is refused BY NAME.** Handing a session id to a CLI with no resume vocabulary would not fail — it would quietly start a NEW conversation, succeed, and archive something that looks right. So a CLI profile, an interactive task and an engine nobody has measured are all refused before anything spawns, each saying which door to use instead.
- **Two writers on one transcript are never opened.** If the Console, a terminal or another task is holding the conversation, the run is created and **parks** with that as its visible reason, then starts by itself when the holder lets go. The row is marked ● in the picker before it is chosen, and the question is asked AGAIN at launch, because minutes pass in between.
- **A resumed run is priced by difference.** The conversation's total is not the task's cost; what this turn added is. On the bridge in the same commit: `tasks.add` takes `resume`, `tasks.get`/`list` give it back, and a retry of a resumed run keeps the same conversation rather than silently opening a fresh one.

**M-10 (the half about WHEN) — three named waits, a strategy for the rest, and a retry that reads the reset hour.** The old policy was one interval and "same or double", so the answer to "when will it try again" was a single number applied to every attempt.

- **The first three waits are three boxes**, not a curve — "five minutes; then twenty; then an hour" is what people actually want out of the first handful of attempts and it is not any curve at all. The 2nd and 3rd are empty by default and say so, which is exactly how every policy written before them still behaves.
- **Retry 4 and after is a strategy:** the same wait, `base × n`, doubling, or **your own formula** in `n` (which retry), `prev` (the wait before it) and `base`, with `+ - * / ^`, brackets, `min()` and `max()`. ‼️ It is PARSED, never evaluated as code: anything outside that grammar is refused in the form, with the reason, next to the person who typed it.
- **‼️ A limit failure waits for the window, not for a number.** With *Limit reset* ticked, a genuine usage/session limit reads the hour the engine itself publishes — the usage endpoint, the rate-limit block, or the "resets …" line the CLI printed — and the retry goes at that moment plus a safety buffer you set. If no reset time can be found the ordinary wait is used and **the run's record says so**; it is never a silent guess.
- The same fields on the bridge (`retry: {delay2Min, delay3Min, backoff, formula, capMin, resetAware, safetyBufferMin}`), validated by the one function the form uses, and `when: "limit"` now means the session/usage limit on any of the four engines rather than Claude's wording alone.

**M-11 — the same hold, at a different number, is the same news.** The budget reason quotes today's spend ("$388.91 of $300.00"), and the notice key was built out of that sentence — so every cent of drift re-announced one unbroken park as a fresh event. What is SHOWN and what IDENTIFIES are now two things: the row and its tooltip keep the live figure, while dedupe works off a stable blocker code (`budget:fleet`, `concurrency:fleet`, `session:busy:<id>`) plus a park-episode number. A ceiling that genuinely changes is a new event; a release followed by the same ceiling later is a new episode and is announced again.

**And the reset-aware tick was drawn wrong.** It asked for a `check` class that does not exist, so the box floated free of its own label — caught in the page render, which is what the render exists for, and put on the same footing as every other checkbox in that form.

## 3.129.0 — The reason a run is held is written where you are looking, and a tooltip you can walk into (2026-08-14)

The V3 audit of 3.128.0 went through six items edge by edge and found every one of them right in the middle and wrong at the rim. This is the rim.

**M-7 (V3) — the extension stopped contradicting itself about the budget.** Three separate things were telling the user three different stories about one held run:

- **The reason is on the row now, under the badge.** The Runs table drew a yellow `parked` and nothing else; the ceiling actually holding the run lived in the status tooltip and in the timeline, so a user who did not hover read one word. The Plan tab had already been taught not to hide an overdue reason and the Runs tab kept the same mistake. Short in the cell, whole in the panel — and it left the Task column, because one fact belongs to one panel.
- **"Nothing was stopped" was the opposite of the truth.** The budget notice still carried a sentence written when the daily limit was a warning line. It became a brake two releases ago, and the notice went on denying the very thing that had held the task — so the board read "nothing was stopped" one row above "did not start — fleet daily budget spent". It now says what the code does, including the box that lets one named run through.
- **One park, one notice.** The manager announced a hold on `task-parked:<id>` and the news scan announced the same hold on `parked:<id>`, so the "say it once" guard could not see they were the same event. Both build the same key now — and the REASON is part of it, so a run held by concurrency this morning and by the budget tonight is two pieces of news rather than one stale one. A hold carried over from a version that did not announce it says so.

**M-3 (V3) — one decision, one write.** Deciding to retry touches two rows: the run that failed and the row that carries the work. They went down as two separate saves, so a crash between them left a failed run promising a retry that did not exist — and the second write swallowed its errors, so a store that refused it produced a row that lived only until the window closed. They are one file now, and a refusal is said out loud. Three smaller lies went with it: the pump used to skip a held run in silence, so a retry whose wait had elapsed hours ago went on saying "waiting until [a time in the past]"; a retry the policy asked for but that had no agent left to run it returned without a word; and the form offered a retry policy for interactive runs, which the core has never applied and cannot.

**X-36 (V3) — the closed-window edit, which is what the cache was built for.** Start-up loaded the persisted snapshot, baselined the projects it had never seen, armed the watcher — and compared nothing. So the exact case the persistence exists for (VS Code closed, a box re-opened and rewritten in Obsidian, VS Code opened again) still recorded no round until the file happened to be touched a second time. Every project the cache already knew is reconciled once at start-up; one baselined a moment ago is skipped, because comparing a file with itself would invent a round out of a fresh install. And `projects.track.restore` now checks that the words handed in actually CONTINUE the fragment recorded at the time — a cut record is a beginning, and anything that does not continue it is a different text being filed as testimony.

**P-14 (V3) — a closing note cut by the old ceiling can be given its words back.** The same append-only repair X-36 got for a round, for the other cut text in the ledger — and the more important one, because the closing note is the single line a person reads three months later. `projects.track.note.restore` writes a correction beside the closing record, never over it; Track and Backloglog apply it on read and mark it recovered.

**S-29 (V3) — one gesture, one repaint, and "ended" is checked.** The host was made the single authority on the repaint last release and the right-click COMMANDS went on calling `refreshIfOpen()` after it, so the menu route still rebuilt the whole conversation twice while the webview's own button rebuilt it once. And the card goes grey before the kill on purpose — the button has to answer immediately — which left the one state it must never reach: a row saying "ended" while the CLI is still holding the session and a later resume is refused with nothing on screen to explain it. The kill is verified a beat later and a survivor is written on the row.

**M-8 (V3) — a tombstone that could not be written is retried.** Both archive divergences ended at a warning: the user was told the board and the archive disagree, and nothing ever tried again. A locked month file is a moment, not a verdict. The failed ids are kept in the store (so the restart that clears the disk does not lose them), retried on the pump's beat, and the warning is TAKEN BACK by the thing that fixed it.

**U-57 — a scrollable tooltip you can actually walk into.** The panel is a child of its wrapper, so on the ordinary layout `:hover` survives the trip into it. `tip-block` — which is what the rows with the most to say use — places the panel against the ROW instead, so reaching it means crossing a strip that is not part of the wrapper: hover ended at the first pixel, the panel's box was collapsed, and a scrollable tooltip became one nobody could scroll. Leaving a wrapper now starts a grace period the length of an ordinary hand movement; arriving inside cancels it, and moving to another row closes the first one at once. It is in the component, so no page has to remember it. The round badge also stopped spending a word's width on two characters: `R3` on the row, `Round 3` as the panel's first line.

## 3.128.0 — A deletion is one transaction, a retry is a row, and the button answers when you press it (2026-08-13)

The V2 audit of 3.127.0 found the same four items still open at their edges, and two more were reopened beside them. This is the six of them, closed at the edges rather than in the middle.

**M-8 (V2) — the live list is inside the transaction too.** Filing the row in the month archive was made the commit point last release, and the other half of a deletion — the list the row disappears from — still went through a catch that called storage best-effort. Three failure paths, each with a test that reproduces it:

- **A follower window is refused at the door.** `save()` was the last thing a deletion did and the only step that noticed the window may not write, so in a second VS Code window the archive line had been appended, the row had left this window's memory and the output file had been unlinked before anybody said "another window owns this list". Every destructive route now asks first, and a refusal costs nothing.
- **The store write throws, atomically.** Temp file plus rename, and the order is: file it → persist the list without it → and only then change memory and delete the output. If the store cannot be written, a tombstone goes back over the archive line so it does not claim a deletion that did not happen, and the run keeps its output.
- **Undo says when the archive did not keep up.** A tombstone that could not be appended was a `console.warn`: the row was back on the board while `tasks.history` went on showing it as deleted, and nobody was told. It is a warning now, naming what diverged.

**P-14 (V2) — the closing-note rule lives on the ledger.** "An agent does not close an item without saying what it did" was enforced by `projects.backlog.check` — the door an agent SHOULD use — while `projects.track { event: "done" }` wrote the same closing event into the same ledger with `note` optional. A rule one op enforces and the next one beside it does not is not a rule; it is checked by whatever writes the record, so every door inherits it and one added later cannot open the hole again. Turn repair got the same treatment: it ran only on rows labelled `turn`, so a row labelled `stale` or `multi` that was ALSO missing its turn was skipped for a reason that says nothing about turns. The gate is the missing fact now; the label only decides which doubt survives.

**X-36 (V2) — the snapshot outlives the window, and a lost round can be given its words back.**

- **The baseline is on disk.** The "what this item said last time we looked" snapshot lived in memory only, so it was empty at exactly the moment the race is likeliest: VS Code has just started, the vault has been open for hours, and the first thing that happens is a box un-ticked in Obsidian with the new round typed straight over the old words. It is written beside the other stores now — a baseline for the next comparison, nothing else reads it, and losing the file costs one round's wording rather than a record.
- **Somebody is watching the backlog file.** A box ticked in the vault produced no event until Agent Farm happened to write something; one debounced watcher per project closes that wait.
- **A round whose wording the old ceiling cut can be completed.** Not by editing the record — the ledger is append-only and a history a surface can rewrite is not a history — but by a correction record pointing at the reopen it completes. Every surface applies the pair and marks the round "recovered later from the file", so recovered words are never mistaken for words captured at the time. On the bridge: `projects.track.restore`.

**A-11 (V2) — the new-profile door obeys the rule the edit door does.** Typing an existing name into "Save this as a profile…" replaced that whole setup with no warning and no undo, while the Edit flow beside it had grown two guards against exactly that. It now says so while you type and asks once, plainly, before it replaces anything — and points at Replace on the row, which is what "save over this setup" is for.

**M-3 (reopened) — a retry is a row on the board, not a closure in a timer.** The policy shipped complete: how many, how long, growing or fixed, which failures, and with what prompt. What it did with the answer was a `setTimeout`. Nothing existed anywhere for forty minutes, nothing was written down, and closing the window between the failure and the timer threw the retry away in silence — while the failed task's own record already said "retried". The default policy is six attempts forty minutes apart, so that was the ordinary case. The retry is now created the moment the failure is, in the queue, carrying the time it may start: it is in the store so a reload keeps it, it is on the board so "what happens now" has a visible answer, and the 30-second pump is the timer — the one every other delay in this extension already trusts.

**S-29 (reopened) — End session and Remove from list answer when you press them.** Both were seconds behind the click, and the order was why: everything ran before the state changed, and the last two steps spawn `taskkill`. Spawning is asynchronous in its result and synchronous in its cost, so the panel heard nothing until Windows had created two processes. The card turns first and the processes are reaped a tick later; `dispose` still kills synchronously, because a kill deferred while the window is closing never happens at all and an orphaned `claude` makes its session unreachable. Removing a live card also repainted the panel twice for one gesture — once for a card that had gone grey and once for a card that was gone. And the Console stopped rebuilding the entire conversation on repaints that carry no new words: up to four hundred rows were re-rendered to flip a status.

## 3.127.0 — The archive is a table, a run that predates the log still has a history, and a reset is a reset again (2026-08-13)

An audit of the previous eleven closures found four items closed before they were finished. This is those four, plus the piece M-8 left behind and one change that was simply wrong and is gone.

**U-41 — reverted, in full.** "Also reset the box height" was asked for as a small addition to a control that already worked. It was implemented by clearing the dragged height along with the five selects, which made the writing box elastic again: from then on it grew and shrank with the column beside it every time a select was picked, and the ↺ itself went dim or live depending on a dimension the user had not asked it to watch. The behaviour is exactly what it was before that change — the height belongs to the writing surface, like the words, and the reset does not touch it. Nothing else about the item was kept.

**M-8 (reopened) — the archive stopped losing runs, and stopped counting them twice.**

- **The archive write is the commit point.** `appendHistory` caught every file error and ignored it while `deleteTask` went on to drop the row, delete the output file and save. A full disk, a permission error or a locked month file was therefore indistinguishable from a successful archive: the row was gone, its output was gone, and the archive it was supposedly filed in had never been written — silent data loss on the one deletion a person makes by hand, usually by accident. It files it FIRST and only then removes it; a failure leaves the row exactly where it was and **says so** rather than looking like a row that quietly went nowhere. Where the archive genuinely is best-effort (overflow pruning, Clear Finished), the caller catches it at its own call site and keeps the rows.
- **One id is one run, whatever the file says twice.** The archive is append-only, so ordinary use files the same run more than once — delete → Undo → delete — and a month file recovered by git can hold a line again. Every reader behind it (success rate, cost, the run counts on the agent pages) was summing those copies. There is now one reading of the archive, the last line about an id wins, and every reader goes through it — the bridge's `tasks.history` included, which parsed the file itself and therefore answered differently from the page.
- **Undo tells the archive too.** Putting a row back writes a tombstone, so a run sitting on the live list is not also listed as filed, and deleting it again files it once.
- **And the runs that already existed have a history.** The event log started the day it was written, so "what happened to this task, from the beginning" answered *nothing at all* for every task already in the store — including the parked one the user was looking at when they asked for it. A task with no record now gets one built on load from what it does carry: when it was made and by what, the ceiling holding it right now, and its start and end. Every rebuilt line is **marked as rebuilt** and the panel says so, because a reconstruction has the times the fields kept and nothing about what happened between them; passing that off as a witnessed record is how a page stops being worth reading for evidence.

**M-9 — "I deleted it and now I need to look at it" is answered on the page.** The data has been there since M-8; the only way to see it was a `.jsonl` opened in the editor — the raw store, for a question the row above it answers in full for every run still on the list. The Runs tab has an **⌛ Archive** switch: the filed runs are drawn in the same table, by the same row, with the same search, filters and sort. They carry their own badge and the word `archived`, and they publish no actions — an archived run offering "cancel" would be a button that cannot mean anything. Once it is on, a month picker appears (all months by default, since a run is remembered by what it was and not by when it left), and `Task history` reads a month INTO the table instead of opening a file. On the bridge in the same commit: `tasks.historyOpen`.

**A-11 (reopened) — a profile loads what it saved, including where it opens.** Saving a profile from the Console recorded nothing about that, so it loaded into a terminal — and the edit form did not offer the choice at all, so there was no way to notice or correct it. **Where it opens** is a dimension of the setup like the engine and the model: it is stored, it is shown on the row, and it is editable. Renaming a profile onto another profile's name silently deleted that other one; the name is now checked and the clash is refused with the name it clashes with.

**X-36 (reopened) — the earlier wording is kept WHOLE, and the row says how many times it has been asked.**

- **`prevText` no longer shares the closing note's 2,000-character ceiling.** A note is something an agent writes and capping it is editorial; the previous round's wording is the USER'S OWN REQUEST, taken off the line a moment before it is overwritten — the last copy that exists anywhere. This project's items run past that routinely, and X-36's own stored round ends mid-sentence, which is where it was caught. It is kept whole, with a real guard an order of magnitude above the longest item seen, and a record that hits it **says** it was cut.
- **Un-ticking the box in Obsidian is caught against a snapshot.** That door produces no event, so the reopen is noticed the next time the file is read — and a user who un-ticked the box AND rewrote the line in one save had the NEW wording stored as the previous round: a lie shaped exactly like the truth. The reconciler now compares against what the line said last time it was read, and where it has no snapshot to compare against the record says the wording is a first sighting rather than a certainty.
- **And the badge is on the backlog row itself.** Reopening an item bumped the round, filed the old wording and stamped the ledger — all of it visible on the Track tab, in `Backloglog.md` and on two bridge ops, i.e. everywhere except the row the person doing it was standing on. The row came back looking untouched, which reads as "nothing happened". It carries the same `round N` badge the Track card does, with every earlier asking dated and whole in the panel.

**P-14 (reopened) — a closing note is not optional, and an unknown turn is not 1.**

- **`projects.backlog.check` refuses to close an item without a note.** Eleven consecutive closures had none, which is the whole reason the audit that found all this had to be commissioned by hand. Closing states now require a real one — checked, not merely asked for.
- **A turn that is not known is not counted as one.** The summary was `Math.max(turns.length, 1)` per session, so a row could print "turns: 1" and "no turn recorded" underneath it at the same time. Sessions touched, turns known and turns unknown are three separate figures now, and nothing is invented.
- **…and most of those unknowns were knowable.** The writer used the session's running TOTAL, which is only right while the last turn is the current one; every turn records when it opened, so the turn a record was written IN can be looked up by its timestamp. Rows already stamped `turn?` are repaired on read where the transcript can still answer, and left alone where it cannot.

**And the kind picker says the form moved.** Changing a draft from a task to a schedule works and has since M-7 — but a task lives on the Runs tab and a schedule on Plan, so the form really does change tabs, and the page repainting with your form somewhere else reads as "it still cannot switch". It now says which tab it moved to and that everything typed came with it. (The kind of a STORED object is still not offered: that would be deleting one thing and making another.)

## 3.126.0 — What happens when a run fails is a setting now, and a part can go to its whole (2026-08-13)

**M-3 — the retry that nobody could see, and nobody could change.** Agent Farm has always retried some failed runs. That was the problem: it did it with numbers written into the manager (six attempts, forty minutes apart), for exactly ONE kind of failure — the Claude session limit, matched against the output with a regular expression — and it kept count by parsing the attempt number back out of the task's **title**. Everything else (a flaky network, a tool that was busy, a push that raced somebody) was final on the first try, at three in the morning, with nobody watching. Retrying is now a policy that rides with the work like every other run dimension, and it answers each question separately:

- **Should it retry at all**, and **how many times** — 0 means never.
- **How long before the first one**, and **how the wait grows after that**: the same interval, or doubling (capped at 12 hours). Those are two different questions and the second one matters — a fixed interval against something that is rate-limiting you is how a retry policy becomes the problem.
- **With the original prompt, or another one.** A retry prompt REPLACES the original rather than being appended, because a retry is a fresh headless run with no memory of the last one; "and this time do it properly" on its own would arrive with nothing to refer to. The form says so where the box is.
- **And for which failures** — a session limit only (the work was never attempted and the limit clears by itself) or any non-zero exit.

It sits on a task, a schedule and a trigger, because the unattended run is the one this exists for; the form's picker has **three** states, not two, since "use my default", "never" and "these numbers" are three different statements and the middle one used to be unsayable. **The shipped default is exactly the old behaviour**, so nothing anybody already has changes. `agentFarm.taskRetry` sets the fleet-wide answer. A retry that did NOT happen now writes a line on the task's own record saying which rule stopped it — that is the case somebody actually goes looking for. On the bridge in the same commit: `retry` on `tasks.add`, `schedules.add`/`edit` and `triggers.add`/`edit`, read back with `retryPolicy` in words; `tasks.get` reports `retryAttempt` and `retryOf`, so a chain of five is followable without reading titles.

**U-56 — a part can be sent to its whole, from the part.** "Gather its parts under it" is pressed on the WHOLE and moves the whole family, which is right when a family has scattered and wrong when you are looking at ONE item that has drifted: it rearranges rows you were not asking about. The child row's own menu now has **Send it under its parent**, and it moves exactly that row — every other entry keeps its place. It lands at the end of the run of parts already sitting behind the whole, so siblings that are already gathered are not pushed down; where the whole has no parts beside it, that is directly behind it. Refused with the reason when the item belongs to nothing or its whole is not in the queue. Like every ordering gesture here it rewrites the 🎯 block and moves no line. On the bridge: `projects.backlog.under`.

**U-52 — the named subsets, in the six places the sweep had missed.** The previous pass found six; these are the rest, and one of them is on seven tabs at once:

- **Toolbox → Scope**: `Agents` — everything that belongs to an agent, not the user-scope entries every agent shares. That is the question this picker is opened with, on every one of its tabs.
- **Tasks → Runs → Status**: `Still going` (running · queued · parked) and `Finished` (done · failed · cancelled). Two readings of one axis, and the page already had the word — "Clear finished" is a button three inches to the right.
- **Tasks → Runs → From**: `Automation` — schedule · trigger · workflow, i.e. everything the farm started by itself rather than the runs you pressed.
- **Tasks → Plan → Kind**: `Automatic` — the three kinds that start on their own, as against the template you launch by hand.
- **Tasks → Runs → Engines**, **Search → Engines** and **Turns → Engines**: `Other engines`, the same subset in the same words the sessions tables use, with the same guard (never offered when there is only one of them, since a click on a row's own name already means "only this").

Nothing was added where the bar is not met: a two-option axis is already one click per name, and a picker built from a list of NAMES has no vocabulary for a subset. `test_presets.js` presses every one of them; `lib_page_dom.js` now routes picker queries through the picker shim for pages that wire their filters through a root rather than the document, which is why three of these were untestable before.

## 3.125.0 — An item remembers being asked before, and a profile loads what it saved (2026-08-13)

**A-12 — the Search page's empty right-hand half was six invisible tooltips.** The page had a horizontal scrollbar under content that fits, and scrolling into it showed nothing, because nothing could be drawn there. `visibility: hidden` hides a box; it does not take it out of layout — and an absolutely positioned box that nothing clips grows the DOCUMENT's scrollable area. Every `AFTip` panel hanging off a trigger in the right-hand half of a page was silently extending it. Measured on Search at an 884px window: `documentElement.scrollWidth` 1384, i.e. **500px of empty page**. A panel that is not open now has no box at all — `min-width` included, because min beats max in CSS and `max-width: 0` on its own would have left 280px standing. The second half of the complaint fixed itself with it: the ✕ tooltips at the right edge were supposed to flip left already (K-18), but the flip measured a panel that had no measurable size yet and gave up before deciding.

**P-14 — the Track page's three silences.**

- **A badge that says which part is uncertain.** Nearly every row wore a bare `uncertain` while naming its session, printing its id and linking to it — which reads as a contradiction, and a warning a reader cannot place is one they learn to ignore. `turn` — by far the commonest — means the session is certain and only which turn of it is not. Every distinct reason is now its own badge (`turn?` · `multi?` · `guess?`), and the panel says what it means.
- **…and there are far fewer of them, because the turn was knowable.** The turn number came from the transcript index, which is a scan; a session asking to be attributed is MID-turn, so the scan that would have counted it has not run. **40 of the 241 rows in this project's ledger were stamped uncertain for that reason alone.** The live count — the hooks counted the prompt before the agent started working — now wins for a live session.
- **The closing note is drawn.** What an agent wrote when it closed an item is the answer to the question this whole tab exists for, and it was never sent to the page: it lived one hover deep inside an event chip that looked exactly like a chip with nothing behind it. It is on the card now, said to be the closer's (the note beside it is the human's, out of `Backloglog.md` — two sources, two colours), long ones open whole in the panel rather than being cut, and a chip that carries a note wears a ✎ so it is worth stopping on. The ledger's silent 300-character cap — which ended a "what to watch out for later" sentence mid-clause — is 2000, and where a note really is longer the cut is **drawn**.

**X-36 — an item that has been asked for before says so, and brings the earlier askings with it.** Reopening an item handed its old text straight back, so the person doing it had to decide by hand, every time, whether to delete it, keep it, or write around it — and whatever they chose, the next agent got one paragraph with no way to tell which round it belonged to. Now the words are **snapshotted at the moment of the reopen**, by whichever door did it (the panel, the bridge, or a box un-ticked in Obsidian, which is the commonest one and is caught by the same reconciliation). So:

- The item's line is free for what is wanted THIS time; nobody preserves anything on purpose. The panel says so where the press was.
- `Backloglog.md` carries `[round:: 3]` on the data line and the earlier askings above the history they produced, numbered and dated.
- The Track card wears a purple `round N` and opens on what each earlier round said.
- **And the agent is HANDED it.** `projects.backlog` and `projects.backlog.next` attach `round` and `earlierRounds` to the item, so "do K-8" can no longer be answered with the thing that already failed twice. Round 1 carries nothing — it is the normal case, and stating it on two hundred lines is a column of noise.

**A-11 — a launch profile now loads what it saved, and editing one means every dimension.** Reopened, and the reasons were exact: the row had been renamed, loading had stopped working, and "editable" had shipped as a rename box.

- **The engine was dropped, on the way in AND on the way out.** A profile saved while the launcher was on Codex stored no engine, so loading it produced a Claude session carrying Codex's model name — the failure furthest in time from the mistake, on the one dimension that decides what every other dimension MEANS.
- **A profile's name is not the session's name**, though they are the same field (`LaunchProfile extends LaunchOptions`, whose `name` is `-n`). Every row also described itself with the launcher's own `describeLaunch`, which printed the profile's name in quotes as if it were a session's.
- **Edit is the launcher's own picker, pointed at a saved row**: name, engine, model, effort, permissions, first prompt — with the model and effort lists reading the DRAFT's engine, so editing a Codex profile is never offered a Claude model. Rename and "replace with what the launcher has" are still there; they were two thirds of an edit, not an edit.
- **The row keeps the name it had** — `Load a profile…`. Loading is what a person comes to it for; the management is on the rows inside, which is what the detail line is for.
- The rules now live in `src/core/launchProfiles.ts` as functions that take a list and return a list, because the reason two of them were wrong for a release is that they lived inside a quickpick loop nothing can call. On the bridge in the same commit: `launch.profile.set` takes `engine` (it could only write profiles that load wrong without it), and every op runs the same core functions the picker does.

## 3.124.0 — A task keeps its own record, and a ceiling that holds it says so (2026-08-13)

**M-7 — its time came and went, and nothing said a word.** A schedule set for 09:00 was still sitting at `parked` at 11:00 with no reason on it, no toast, no log line, and a Plan row cheerfully drawing *next tomorrow 09:00*. Four separate silences, fixed at the four places that knew:

- **The window that cannot fire says so.** Only ONE VS Code window holds the queue lease; in every other one the scheduler tick returns immediately and writes nothing. The Plan tab — the page whose entire subject is automation — now opens with a banner when this is that window, and an overdue row points at it.
- **A row whose minute has passed wears `overdue`, and says why underneath it.** The row is asked the same questions the tick asks (is this window the runner · is the gate blocking · does the guard say there is nothing to do · has the tick simply not come round yet) and answers on its own line, in words. Not in a hover: a fact you have to hover for is the same failure one layer in.
- **A held run is not announced as a started one.** `fireSchedule` said "scheduled task started" and *then* asked for the launch, so the one message you got was wrong on exactly the occasions it mattered, and the automation log recorded a clean `fired` over the top of it. The launch is attempted first now; the toast, the log line and Run Now all report the answer it gave. An unattended run that could not start also puts a notice on the board — once, keyed on the task.
- **"Run it even if the daily budget is spent."** The budget is a brake with no release, which is right for a runaway automation and wrong for the one run that has to happen anyway — and it is the user's own ceiling about money. There is a box for it on a task, a schedule and a trigger, and it is deliberately narrow: it does NOT lift the 5-hour quota guard or the concurrency limits, which protect the plan window and the machine. The label says so where the box is.

**And the kind is a field, not a door you had to pick before you started typing.** A task and a schedule differ by one thing — whether a clock starts it — so realising halfway through that this should run every morning meant closing the form, losing the prompt and starting again on the other tab. One picker, and the draft survives the change. Changing the kind of something already SAVED is not offered: that would be deleting one thing and making another. And **"make another like this one" now copies the kind**: a run a schedule produced clones back into a schedule, with its time, its day, its missed policy and its guard — it used to drop all of it and open a plain task with no time field at all.

**M-8 — the run table knew thirty facts about a run and drew eight.**

- **A Session column.** A task that ran opened a session; its transcript was matched minutes later and the only thing done with that fact was to put a price in the Cost column. The id is drawn, in monospace so two can be compared, and clicking it opens the conversation.
- **Every column is a hover now, one subject each, no fact in two of them.** The TASK carries the whole prompt (it was cut at 600 characters into a `<pre>`), the STATUS carries **what happened to the run, in order, stamped**, FROM carries the automation's whole setup, SESSION carries the conversation and its figures, and Took carries its own derivations.
- **‼️ A task keeps its own record.** Three timestamps and a status cannot answer "its time came and went — what happened?": a run held at a ceiling, released, held again and finally started leaves exactly the same three fields as one that simply sat there, and the reason it was held was overwritten by the next reason. Every transition writes a line at the moment it happens, next to the code that decides it — which is the only place that knows why. Capped, and it travels into the archive with the task.
- **Clicking a run's title opens the form it was made with, read-only.** The same card, the same fields, the same order, every control dead — not a paraphrase in a detail grid that would be a second thing to keep in step.
- **The triangle says the whole anatomy.** It used to say the word "schedule" and stop, which is the one thing the row above it already said. Every dimension that was STATED is listed — for a schedule, a trigger, a template and a run alike.
- **A deleted row is filed, not burned, and ↶ Undo puts it back.** Two ways off the list already archived the task; the third — the ✕, the one a hand presses by accident — simply dropped it. So the only route a PERSON takes was the only one with no way back and no record. It goes to the same monthly archive as the others, which is also what Analytics reads. (Its output file is gone either way, and the restored row says so instead of offering a dead button.)

**On the bridge**, in the same commit: `ignoreBudget` is a run dimension like the rest — readable and writable on `tasks.add`, `schedules.add`/`edit` and `triggers.add`/`edit`, and it survives a round trip. `tasks.add` now returns whether the run actually **started** and what **held** it. `tasks.get` returns the run's `events`. `tasks.history` reads the archived RUNS when given a month, which is what "what was that task I deleted" needs.

## 3.123.0 — The hover has a name, and the Console's cards use it (2026-08-13)

**U-55 — it is called `AFTip`, and the principle behind it is written down.** The scrollable panel had no name, so asking for it meant describing it ("our new advanced scrollable tooltip") on every backlog item, every time — and an agent who was not told simply used a native `title`. There are exactly two hover surfaces in this extension and now they are named: **`AFTip`** (`media/afTip.js`) carries anything, because it is as wide as it needs, it scrolls, and the pointer can travel into it; a native `title` carries a handful of figures and nothing else. **Anything that is TEXT, or that could grow into text, is an `AFTip`.**

Above it sits the rule the whole layout serves, which had also never been stated: **maximum information in minimum space, at minimum clicks.** A fact a reader can see must not cost them a click — the click leaves the page and takes their place, their scroll position and the comparison they were making with it. The worked example to copy is the sessions table, where every column and every figure carries what you would otherwise open the session for. Both are in `docs/CONVENTIONS.md` and in the agent-side `_REF/agentfarm-dev.md`, so neither has to be asked for again.

**U-50 — the Console's cards are read without opening anything.** A card is 250px wide and shows six facts about a conversation that has forty; everything it cut was reachable only by opening another page. Each card now answers **four questions on hover, and they do not overlap**: the identity line says WHO and WHERE (session id whole, engine, the model and effort actually answering, permissions, folder, project), the name says WHAT THIS CONVERSATION IS (the name whole, turns, when it last moved, held messages), the figure says WHAT IT HAS SPENT (raw, weighted, money, per-turn, the context the next turn starts from), and the phase says WHAT IS HAPPENING RIGHT NOW (since when, what the running turn is on, the question it is parked on, the tool waiting for permission). The card's own `title` is gone: a native tooltip on an object that also has panels races them, and which one you got depended on which pixel you stopped on.

**And the Resume list, which had been left behind.** Every row carried one native `title` naming its folder — on the surface whose whole job is choosing between a hundred conversations by what they were. Its rows now open on the session's **opening prompt**, with what it did under it (turns · tool calls · errors · models · your labels · when it started), and a row a live process already owns says so where the click is offered. Nothing costs a second query: all of it was already in the index and simply never sent to the page.

**U-53 — a swim lane says which session it is drawing.** "developer #1 / developer #2" tells you which of an agent's rows you are on and not which conversation, on the one page whose entire job is watching several at once. The id now sits under the agent's name, in the editor's own monospace so ids can be read against each other. A row that is already labelled by its id gets no second line repeating it; a packed row (several sessions in sequence) names its current one and says how many more it carries; a session the engine has not named yet says that rather than printing nothing.

**A-13 — the bar above the backlog breaks where it is told to.** Grouping the buttons was the first half of this and it was not enough: the whole right-hand group still travelled between lines as the panel resized, so the bar had two shapes and you met whichever one the window happened to produce. The break is declared now. First line: the counts at the left, and the four things that REWRITE the file (Tidy · Gather · Archive all closed · Archive older) at the right. Second line: the doors ONTO the file, at the left. Same on Track's bar, which has the same six buttons.

## 3.122.3 — The named subsets, everywhere a list has a word for one (2026-08-13)

**U-52.** `presets` has been part of `AFFilter.multi` since the Console needed `Dialog`, and for three versions exactly one picker in the extension used it — a preset is per-picker DATA and the component deliberately does not know what any of the words mean, so nothing ever prompts a page to declare one. A sweep of every picker found five more subsets that have a name:

- **Track → state**: `Active` · `Passive`. It is the same axis as the backlog's, so it is the same constant rather than the same idea typed twice — a subset named on one tab and not on its neighbour is the reader learning a gesture and then losing it.
- **Backlog → family**: `In a family` — both ends of one, parts and wholes, nothing that stands alone.
- **Notices → level**: `Urgent` — the top two, Ultra and Important.
- **Notices → status**: `Settled` — answered and declined, nothing still waiting on you. (`Waiting` is one option, and a click on its own name has always given you that.)
- **Queue → authors**: `Agents` — everything the fleet put there and nothing you wrote yourself.
- **Sessions tables → engines**: `Other engines` — everything that did not run on the default, which is the question a four-engine list is read with.

**And a bar, written down, so the row does not fill up with decoration**: the subset needs a name somebody would use out loud, it must be two options or more (a single option is already one click, on its own name), and a picker built from a list of NAMES — agents, projects, targets — has no vocabulary for a subset to be named out of. Each one is only offered when it would actually save a press: the queue's `Agents` and the table's `Other engines` appear only once there is more than one of them.

The failure this ships with a guard against is the one the backlog itself had: a `presets` declared in a page's list and dropped on the way to the component by a call site that never forwarded it — it draws nothing and says nothing. `test_presets.js` presses every one, and `lib_page_dom.js` now exposes the picker shim so any page test can.

## 3.122.2 — Active and Passive, one click each, on the backlog's state picker (2026-08-13)

**U-51.** The four states are really two questions — what is still work, and what is finished with — and either one cost a **Clear** plus two ticks, on the axis this tab is filtered by more than any other. `Active` (open · doing) and `Passive` (done · cancelled) now sit between **Select all** and **Clear**, exactly where the Console's `show` box puts `Dialog`.

Nothing new was invented for it: `presets` has been part of `AFFilter.multi` since the Console needed one, and the component deliberately does not know what any of the words mean — a named subset is the picker's own data. What was missing was the backlog passing them through.

## 3.122.1 — The bar above a project document breaks in one place, and the column of names stops taking the width (2026-08-13)

**A-13.** Six buttons in one right-aligned run above the backlog did not fit, so whichever ones happened to overflow dropped to a second line — a different set at every panel width, with no way to tell what belonged with what. There are two groups now, with the flexible gap between them: **the doors ONTO the file sit beside the file's own name**, and the things that REWRITE it (Tidy · Gather · Archive all closed · Archive older) keep the right-hand end they had. Each run is a flex box of its own, so it is placed whole and only splits internally once it truly cannot fit — which means the bar breaks in one predictable place instead of shedding a button. The same shape is applied to all four of these bars, including Track's, which had the same six-in-a-row problem.
