**‼️ THE SECOND ENGINE EXISTED AND COULD ONLY BE REACHED FROM THE BRIDGE (X-18).** 3.101.0 taught the Console to speak Codex; every button in the extension still went to `claude` without a word — which is the same fault the run-surface had before it grew a picker, one layer down. The engine is now a dimension of a launch, offered on every surface that starts one, from a single vocabulary: the Console composer, ▶ on a sidebar row, the full **Start Session…** launcher, the New-task form (and therefore schedules and triggers, which are tasks that have not happened yet), a mission's delegations with a per-member override, and the bridge. `agentFarm.launchDefaults.engine` sets the answer once. **Nothing has to be chosen between them:** a Claude session and a Codex session run side by side in the same window, and every row says which is which.

**One refusal, worded once.** `engineRefusal()` is the single sentence five entry points share, and an engine that cannot run **stays in the list** — hiding it makes "this build has no Codex" and "your Codex is not signed in" look identical — offered disabled, carrying the one thing to do about it. The check runs again on the host's own side of every webview, and again before a queued task spawns: a task launched last week on an engine since uninstalled now fails with that sentence instead of writing "command not found" into an archive somebody opens hours later.

**‼️ A BADGE WAS LYING ABOUT THE PROJECT BRIEF — measured against `codex --help`.** That engine has **no system-prompt channel**: prompt, `AGENTS.md`, config, nothing else. So a Codex session started under a project was filed under it, wore its badge, and had never been told. The brief now rides inside the **first prompt**, fenced and consumed once. A *terminal* session on that engine is deliberately not told and says so — typing into the user's own terminal on their behalf is not this extension's job.

**A task on a per-turn engine is a different command, not different flags** (measured, one real run): plain `codex exec` prints its own header — workdir · model · provider · approval · sandbox · reasoning effort · session id — then the conversation and `tokens used`, which is exactly the header an archive wants, written by the engine itself. The prompt travels on **stdin**, which is also what lets it carry the brief and its own newlines. `agentFarm.taskArgs`, the model-policy rules and `outputContract: "json"` are Claude's vocabulary and are **not** passed to a binary that has never heard of them — the archive's header says so where a reader will find it.

**‼️ $0.00 IS NOT A SMALLER NUMBER, IT IS A WRONG ONE (X-19).** Codex runs against a plan and states no per-turn price; the adaptor already refused to invent one, but every surface still printed dollars. Everything about such a session is now in **tokens**, with the reason beside it: the running turn (no "~" — the tokens are exact), the session line, the card hover, the line that closes a turn, the transcript's own result row, and the bridge (`reportsCost` on `tasks.list` / `tasks.add`, `tokens` on `missions.status`). Nothing is estimated for it either: our rate card is Anthropic's, and running it over a ChatGPT plan's tokens would be wrong twice over.

**…and a cost ceiling means nothing on such an engine, which is why missions grew a second currency.** `maxCostUSD` is the only mission limit with real teeth — rounds and tasks are coarse, minutes only bite on something slow. A plan-billed delegate never moves it, so a team of them could burn an entire plan window against a $5 ceiling that can never be reached: not a cap, the appearance of one. `MissionLimits.maxTokens` (default 2 000 000, `0` = none, so an existing mission behaves identically) is checked alongside it, whichever runs out first stops the run, and the **planner is told** — a lead that only ever saw "$5.00 left" would plan as if nothing had been spent.

**The planner itself stays on Claude Code, and that is a decision.** A mission's whole control flow parses `claude -p --output-format json` — the decision object, the cost, the session id that carries the lead's context from round to round — and spends `--disallowedTools` and `--max-turns`, neither of which exists on the other engine. Nobody has measured an equivalent, and this repo does not ship a translation it has not run.

**Looked at, not assumed.** A Console fixture with two live engines on screen was rendered and photographed, dark and light: the badge on the busiest card read **"C…"** — the one row where knowing the engine matters most was the one row that could not say it. It is a sibling of the name now with `flex:none`, and the session id gives up the space instead. The renderer also learned to put a page's slot content back inside its shell, without which the Console could not be photographed at all.

## 3.101.0 — A second engine actually speaks: Codex runs in the Console, measured turn by turn (2026-08-10)

**‼️ THE CONSOLE IS NO LONGER `claude` HARD-CODED (X-15, phases 1–2).** A hosted session can now run on **Codex**, and it does so through the panel, the state, the cost strip, the step icons and the transcript that were already there — because the second engine is a **translation**, not a second Console. `src/core/codexproto.ts` turns Codex's `thread.started` / `turn.started` / `item.*` / `turn.completed` into the events `reduceEvent` has always understood. Not one field name is shared between the two streams, which is precisely why doing it any other way would have meant forking the extension.

**Everything below was measured against codex-cli 0.147.0 on this machine, on a ChatGPT plan with no API key anywhere. Three of the measurements changed the design, and one of them is the whole reason it works:**

**‼️ A TURN IS NOT OVER WHEN THE ENGINE SAYS IT IS.** `turn.completed` is printed, the answer is complete — and `codex exec` then lives on for another **~5.4 seconds**, holding the thread's writer lock the entire time. A turn started in that window is refused outright: `thread-store conflict: thread <id> already has an active writer`. The first end-to-end run lost its second message to exactly this. So a per-turn session is held at *working* until its **process** is gone, not until its output is: `acceptUserText` only honours a hold while the status is working — an idle session starts a turn whatever the caller asked for — so that one line is what makes the queue, the release and the whole transport correct rather than racing. A late `close` from the previous turn's process is ignored rather than read as the current turn's death.

**‼️ `codex exec resume` AND `codex exec` DO NOT SHARE A FLAG VOCABULARY.** `resume` refuses `-s` and `-C` — exit code 2, not a warning. The sandbox travels as `-c sandbox_mode=…`, which resume does accept (confirmed under `--strict-config`, which errors on any key it does not know, so the key is real rather than tolerated), and the working directory is the process's own cwd. A resume built like a first turn simply fails, which is the kind of thing that is otherwise discovered in production.

**‼️ AND THE PROMPT GOES ON STDIN.** Given as an argument the CLI *still* drains stdin when stdin is a pipe — "Reading additional input from stdin…" — and waits for a second copy of the message that never comes. An explicit `-` says where the instruction is coming from.

**What a per-turn engine changes, said out loud instead of discovered.** A message typed mid-turn **steers** a Claude turn and is **held** by a Codex one, whose process is the turn and whose stdin is already closed — accepting it and losing it was not an option, so it is queued, which the composer already draws. Model, effort and permission mode are not control requests here but the **next turn's flags**, so they are applied at once rather than left pending on an answer that can never arrive. A permission mode becomes a **sandbox chosen up front**, because `codex exec` has nobody to ask: `default` — Claude's "ask me" — becomes read-only, which is the only honest thing an engine that cannot ask can do, and the surfaces say so rather than letting the same word carry an assumption it cannot keep.

**‼️ AND NO DOLLAR FIGURE IS INVENTED.** Codex runs on a plan and states no price for a turn. The result line carries **no cost** rather than a zero that would read as "this turn was free"; tokens are counted, and `input_tokens` has the cached share **subtracted** from it first — Codex counts cache inside the input and Claude counts it beside, so passing the number through would have counted the context twice (measured: 11 008 of a 14 310-token turn).

**On the bridge, in the same commit.** `console.start` takes `engine`, **refuses** one this machine cannot run — with the one thing to do about it, which `engines.status` already knows — and answers with the shape of the session it opened (`turnShape`, `steerable`, `reportsCost`) so a caller does not learn the difference by watching a message disappear. `test/smoke_codex_console.mjs` drives the real host against the real engine: two turns, one thread, the context carried.

**The design doc was wrong and is corrected.** 3.99.0 recorded the usage fields as `cached_input` / `cache_write_input` / `output` / `reasoning_output` and omitted `input_tokens` entirely — not a typo but a mapping that would not have worked. `docs/MOTORLAR-TASARIM.tr.md` now carries the measured names, the cache-inclusion trap, the per-turn item ids, and the Kaspersky section kept deliberately as a method note: it is the proof that a screen saying "ready" can still be lying, and only a real call finds it.

## 3.100.0 — A code looks like a code, a hover opens where it belongs, and the work that closed an item is found again (2026-08-10)

**‼️ ONE MISSING QUOTE HID A BADGE ON EVERY ITEMS CARD, FOR FOUR RELEASES.** `title="…Turns per session: 2.0>⊞ 1 · ⤳ 2 · 3h</span>` — the attribute never closed, so the browser swallowed the summary it was supposed to describe along with the rest of the row. Every string assertion passed, because the string was there; nothing had ever drawn it. The `⊞ sessions · ⤳ turns · span` figures are back on every card, and the page's tests now assert the invariant rather than the symptom: **no attribute may contain markup**, on any of the four views.

**‼️ A BACKLOG CODE IS THE SAME OBJECT WHEREVER IT IS DRAWN (A-5).** The code is the one permanent handle this whole record is indexed by, and it read as two different things on two tabs of the same page: a chip on the Backlog, a dotted-underlined word on the Track. One CSS rule now carries both selectors so they cannot drift again, and one function draws every code on all four Track views — same chip, same click affordance, and a hover that carries **the item itself** instead of a sentence about codes in general. The only part that differs between views is the line saying what pressing it does, which has to differ: it opens the backlog line on Items and narrows the list on Timeline and Gaps.

**‼️ A HOVER PANEL OPENS WHERE THE ROW IS, NOT WHERE THE POINTER IS.** Anchored to its trigger, a 760px panel hung off a chip three quarters of the way across the panel grows straight off the right edge — the half you wanted to read is the half that is not there. This was solved once on the backlog row and never generalised; it is now `tip-block` in `common.css`, the shared component's own rule: the wrapper goes `position:static`, so the panel is measured against the **row or card** and opens at its left edge, in its width, always in the same place. Every rich hover on the Track uses it, and a test refuses any that does not.

**‼️ AN INLINE BOX IGNORES `overflow`, AND THAT QUIETLY UNDID EVERY WIDTH RULE ON THE TIMELINE.** The note column carried `nowrap` + `overflow:hidden` + ellipsis and was obeying none of them: wrapping it for its hover panel had made it an inline span inside a block. So the note was not ellipsised, it was drawn — all of it, on one line — and the row grew until the whole page scrolled sideways and dragged the Items cards out with it. The clipping now belongs to the wrapper, which is the flex child. On an Items card the item's words **wrap to the card** and `more` stays where it can be reached; opened past the cut, the text stays inside the same block instead of running to the right of the window.

**‼️ A TOOLTIP THAT SAYS THE SAME THING ON TWO HUNDRED ROWS IS FURNITURE.** The event chip carried three paragraphs in a native tooltip: the record's figures, then the definition of the event, then the definition of the source — and both definitions are already stated once, on the filter chips above the list, which is where a vocabulary belongs. What is left on a row is that row's own: figures in the native tooltip, and the one thing that is genuinely TEXT — the note somebody wrote — in the rich panel. The same sweep took the repeated explanations off the work-type chips, the state badge, the family badges and the closing stamp. The test now bans an essay in a row-level `title`, while leaving the page's controls alone: a control's own hover is where that control is defined, once.

**‼️ THE SESSION ID IS A DOOR.** It sat on two views as a piece of text with the whole session described in a tooltip — the one thing a reader wants from a session id, the transcript, was the one thing it did not do, while the turn chips beside it did. It opens now, at the moment the item was worked there. The turn chip says `Turn N of M` and what a turn *is* here (one instruction and everything done about it), because `#1` on a session that ran for hours is honest and read as a placeholder nobody had filled in.

**‼️ THE WORK THAT CLOSED AN ITEM IS FOUND AGAIN, EVEN WHEN THE TICK WAS A CLICK.** Closing an item in the Backlog panel is honestly `by: panel` with no session — the click happened in no conversation, and the ledger must not pretend otherwise. But the question the closing stamp exists to answer still had an answer, and the ledger was already holding it. Measured on the real project: four items closed this way, each with a full agent-recorded history minutes earlier, carried **no stamp at all** and appeared under *no session to point at* — the record looked as though it had lost the day's work. `closingAttribution` now falls back to the last run that had already started when the item closed, and says `carried` rather than passing the pointer off as observed: the ledger keeps saying the click had no session, because that is true, and the stamp points at the turn where the work happened, because that is also true. A run that began *after* the close is never credited — that is later work on something reopened. `Backloglog.md` carries `[uncertain:: carried]` and its legend explains it, and the Gaps view's own rows finally carry the code hover they were missing.

## 3.99.0 — Engines get their own page, and a button that proves a call actually gets through (2026-08-10)

**‼️ "READY" AND "ACTUALLY WORKS" ARE DIFFERENT QUESTIONS, AND ONLY ONE OF THEM CAN BE ANSWERED BY READING A FILE (X-14).** The engine matrix reads disk — is the CLI on PATH, is there a grant in its credentials file — and this machine is the proof that both can be true while nothing runs: `codex login status` reports *Logged in using ChatGPT* and every turn dies at the socket (`os error 10013`, local security software refusing `codex.exe`). No amount of reading finds that. So the engines now have **their own page** (`Agent Farm: Open Engines`, and a button in the Farm Settings section that is now the summary of it), and on it each engine carries a **⚡ Test**: one real turn, the engine's smallest model, a four-word prompt, and read-only sandboxing where the engine offers it — a health check that can touch a file is not a health check. **It costs a turn, so it never runs by itself:** not on a schedule, not on page open, not on a refresh, and a second press while one is in flight does not buy a second turn. The verdict is cached for the life of the window and shown **with its age**, because a tick from four hours ago is not the same claim as one from four seconds ago; a failure prints the **engine's own words** rather than an invented reason. On the bridge as `engines.test`, which refuses to spend on an engine that is not installed. The placement was the user's to make and was made on 2026-08-09 (`docs/MOTORLAR-TASARIM.tr.md`); Blueprint has an **Engines** section in both languages.

**Codex, measured end to end (X-15 groundwork).** The CLI was installed (`0.147.0`) and the sign-in **works** — so the answer to X-3's real question is yes: a ChatGPT plan runs Codex with no API key anywhere. Its streaming protocol was then measured rather than guessed, from a live `codex exec --json` and from the binary's own tags: `thread.started` · `turn.started` · `turn.completed` (usage in tokens) · `turn.failed` · `item.started/updated/completed` · `error`, with item types `agent_message` · `reasoning` · `command_execution` · `file_change` · `mcp_tool_call` · `web_search` · `todo_list`. **Not one field name is shared with Claude Code's `stream-json`**, so the Console adaptor is a real translation layer and the design doc now carries the mapping table — plus the two differences that change it: Codex states **no cost** for a turn (it runs on a plan and counts tokens only), and its approval flow is its own. The adaptor is deliberately **not** written until a real turn can be seen on this machine.

**One gesture, honestly named.** Install and Sign in do exactly one thing — write a line into a terminal **without running it** — so they are one function (`engineTerminal`) shared by the Settings section and the panel. It was called `engineLogin` while already carrying an install command; a handler whose name is a lie is the next reader's bug.

**The looking glass got two fixes of its own.** `render_page.js` drew `#app` plus every element the shim had ever handed out, which drew some pages twice; and a full-page screenshot repeats `position: sticky` elements where the viewport would have ended, which reads as the page having rendered twice. Both are now handled in the renderer, so a picture is evidence about the page rather than about the tool.

## 3.98.0 — The colours were chosen without ever drawing the page; a tab strip that reads as a row of files; and an engine that is signed in but not installed (2026-08-09)

**‼️ NOTHING IN THIS REPO EVER DREW A PAGE, AND IT SHOWED.** The page scripts run in a webview, the tests read their markup as strings, and between writing a CSS rule and shipping a VSIX nobody looked. So 3.97.0 shipped a ten-tab strip in which four tabs repeated two hues, a Timeline whose event chip sat in a 92px box to say `done`, a session id column 120px wide for twelve characters, and a palette that painted the **majority** — every closed record filled solid green — which is the same as painting the background. All of it passed every string assertion in the suite; all of it was wrong in one look. **`test/render_page.js`** now runs the real page script through the tests' own DOM shim, wraps its markup in `media/common.css` plus a stand-in for the editor's theme variables, and writes a self-contained HTML file; **`test/shoot_page.js`** photographs it with a headless Chrome already on the machine — no browser download, a throwaway profile, the user's own windows untouched. Fixtures live in `test/fixtures/` and their **shape** is the point: a fixture with one open item and one closed one flatters any palette, so the Track one carries a real ledger's distribution — mostly closed, a few open, one being worked, one cancelled, one whose backlog line is gone. It asserts nothing and is not in `npm test`: a page that looks right proves the CSS, never the behaviour. `docs/CONVENTIONS.md` → *Looking at it* makes reading the picture part of a visual change being done.

**‼️ THE PROJECT TAB STRIP IS A ROW OF FILES IN A DRAWER (U-25).** Every sub-page is reached from that one row, and it read as one more line of metadata between the summary above it and the table below — 3.97.0 gave it colour only in a hairline under the selected tab, which is a colour system nobody can see. Each of the ten tabs now has a **grip**: the coloured strip along its top edge, its own faint tint in the body, its own frame. The selected one comes forward, takes the page's own background, grows a thicker grip and cuts through the rule the row sits on — in front, not merely marked. Six hues are the editor's own chart colours and the four between them are mixed from those (teal for Sessions, earth for Docs, gold-green for Cost), so ten tabs stay distinguishable while still following the user's theme; Settings is deliberately neutral, because it is not one of the readings but the way out of them.

**The Track's colour scale is by attention, not by sentiment.** A real project's ledger is mostly closed work, so filling every `done` chip solid green turned the page into one flat green and made the two rows that need an eye exactly as loud as the eighty that do not. A **fill** now means still moving or gone wrong (doing, reopen); an **outline in its own hue** means settled and still recognisable at a glance (done, cancelled); the quiet majority (touch) carries no hue. Closed cards keep a fainter left rule. Each item also carries its **work type** in the backlog's own colours — the one palette the two pages deliberately share, because a `#bug` is the same red wherever it is drawn. The **Gaps** view was entirely grey whether a section held forty problems or none: each card is now green when its own hole is closed, and its own hue when it is not — amber for history nobody recorded, yellow for a best-effort attribution, purple for a reconstructed row, blue for the records that have no session **on purpose**, which is not a fault and must not be drawn as one.

**‼️ A COLUMN IS AS WIDE AS ITS OWN WIDEST VALUE.** Every Timeline width was a round number typed by hand — sized by guessing rather than by looking at what goes in it, and it wasted exactly the room the note column needed. They are **measured at render time** from the rows being drawn (`trkTlWidths`) and arrive as character counts, so the columns still line up — which is the whole reason to fix them — without paying for text nobody has.

**‼️ AN ENGINE CAN BE SIGNED IN AND NOT INSTALLED, AND THIS MACHINE IS (X-3).** `~/.codex/auth.json` holds a live ChatGPT grant while `codex` is on no PATH and in no global npm tree — an account with nothing to run it. 3.97.0 read the credentials file, called that "a plan", and offered **Sign in with a plan** for a command that does not exist. That is the "not configured" this whole section was built to replace, in a new costume. The row now names the state it is actually in: *not installed on this machine*, the install line on the row (`npm install -g @openai/codex`), and — when the grant is already on disk — *the sign-in is already on disk, so this is the only step left*. **Install** is the primary button while the CLI is missing and the sign-in appears only once there is something to sign in to; the key box stays either way, since a key is storable before the CLI arrives. Install and sign-in are one binding (`engineCommand`), because they do one thing: write a line into a terminal **without running it**. The handler was called `engineLogin` while already carrying something that was not a login.

## 3.97.0 — End session ends it, the project comes back with the session, a Track tab you can order and read, and the engines say how they are paid for (2026-08-09)

**‼️ END SESSION ENDED NOTHING, TWICE OVER (K-12).** The button was pressed, the card flipped to *ended*, and seconds later the agent was still writing — so it was pressed again, and again. Two faults behind one symptom. **The kill order:** `child.kill()` ended the `shell:true` wrapper on the spot while `taskkill /PID p /T` was still only a queued spawn, and taskkill resolves `p` **before** walking the tree — so a `p` that died a millisecond ago is "process not found" and the real `claude` underneath was never touched, orphaned but still holding the pipe this window reads. The tree kill goes first and alone now; the plain kill is the fallback for when taskkill cannot start. **The drain:** killing is not instant and whatever the CLI had already written was still in the pipe, so those lines went on through `reduceEvent`, which read them as an ordinary running turn and moved `status` off `ended` — the button flipped back to *End session* and the session looked alive again. A session you ended is now detached before the kill and nothing is read, written or reduced from it afterwards. Pressing it a second time still re-issues the kill: a second press means "that did not take", and turning the last resort into a dead button is the one thing this fix must not do.

**‼️ THE PROJECT WAS LOST IN TWO DIFFERENT PLACES (K-11).** *On resume:* the Console's **own** resume was the single route that handed `ConsoleHost.start` no project, so it fell back to the window's active one — which is not that session's answer and is normally none at all. The conversation came back filed nowhere and was re-told the wrong brief, or none. It passes the session's own project now, through the same membership order every other resume route already used. *On start:* the chip above the composer and the picker beside it were **one field**. `start` clears the picker on purpose — the next session picks its own project — so the instant you pressed **Start session** the chip read "No project" about a session that had just been started under one. Nothing had been lost; the strip was answering the wrong question. They are two fields now: the picker answers for the session about to start, the chip for the one on screen. A session running under a project that has since been finished is still named, off its own row.

**‼️ THE TRACK TAB'S ORDERING REACHED ONE VIEW OUT OF FOUR (U-25).** "Last worked" and "first worked" produced the same page because on three of the four views neither was being read: `trkOrder` was called by the Items view and by nothing else, while the dropdown and the ↑↓ button were drawn on all of them. Every axis now declares which views it can honour, each view offers only those, and the ones it offers work. The **Sessions** cards order by last, first, turns, span or closed; the **Gaps** list by code or area; the **Timeline** offers no axis and says *chronological* instead of a dropdown that cannot change anything — but its ↑↓ is real, which is the half that was silently dead there. A choice a view cannot answer is not overwritten, only set aside: switch to Sessions and back and "state" is still yours.

**And the Track tab has colour, on three axes and no more.** The event chip is **filled** by kind — done green, cancelled red, started blue, reopened yellow, a plain touch left outlined because it is most of the ledger and a list where every row shouts says nothing. Every card and row carries a 3px left rule in its **state's** colour. A history that was reconstructed rather than watched wears the purple the backlog gives research. A session card that closed something reads differently from one that only touched things, and each code chip on it is coloured by what that run did to it. **The Timeline's columns were given their widths:** the row is a flex line, so a column with no basis shrank below its own content and wrapped inside its box — the clock broke over two lines, the code broke after the dash, the session id broke before the turn number, and a one-line record drew as three. Nothing shrinks now except the note, which ellipsises with the whole text in a rich hover. The words on a Timeline row hang off `AFTip` instead of a native `title` that repeated the row and then cut, and the last column's panel opens **back across the row** rather than off the right edge (`tip-right`, new in `common.css`).

**The project page's tab strip is the handle the whole page is operated by, and it was drawn as one more line of grey.** Each of the ten tabs carries its own hue — in a permanent hairline underline, in the hover, and in the selected tab's tint and rule. A tint and 2px, not a block of paint: the page below is what is being read.

**‼️ THE ENGINES SAY HOW THEY ARE PAID FOR — AND TWO OF THREE ARE NOT PAID FOR WITH A KEY (X-3, phase 1).** Everything the extension knew about a non-Claude engine was "is there a key in the vault", and the vault is a flat name→value table that knows nothing about engines — so *"why can I not run Codex here"* had no surface at all and the answer was Ctrl+Shift+P → Set Secret plus a guess at which name to type. The measured truth is different: **Claude Code runs on a Pro/Max plan** (`~/.claude/.credentials.json` → `claudeAiOauth`) and **Codex runs on a ChatGPT plan** (`~/.codex/auth.json` → `auth_mode: "chatgpt"`, `OPENAI_API_KEY: null` — read off this machine, 2026-08-09); both keep their own OAuth grant in their own file and never hand it over. Gemini is the one that really is a key now, because its "sign in with Google" route was retired in June 2026 — a dated fact the row states, so nobody hunts for a login that was taken away. **Farm Settings → Engines** answers four questions per engine: is the CLI here, how is it paid for, is it signed in, and the one thing to do next. No row stops at naming a state. Three gestures and none of them touches a credential: the sign-in is **typed** into a terminal and not run (starting a browser login is the user's to do), the key route hands over to the vault's own form on that exact name, and the docs link is `https` only. On the bridge as **`engines.status`**, with a test that scans the wire for `access_token`/`refresh_token` and fails if either appears. Design and the remaining phases: `docs/MOTORLAR-TASARIM.tr.md`; the report on whether a subscription can replace an API key for the other engines is in the vault and on Notices.

## 3.96.0 — The Track tab stops being a sketch: four views over one ledger, and a page that says what it does not know (2026-08-09)

**‼️ TRACK, AT THE BACKLOG TAB'S CALIBRE (P-5).** The join between a permanent code and the turns it was fought in existed as a file nobody read and a tab that listed it. It is now four views over **one narrowing**, so a filter set in one still holds in the next — a view switch is a different question, not a different page. **⬢ Items**: a card per code, its sessions and turns oldest first, the closing note somebody wrote for it, and the one figure that needs both files open — the size the backlog estimated against the turns it actually took, with **the median for that size in this project** beside it (stated only once there are enough sized items for a median to mean anything, because "twice the median" computed from two numbers is noise dressed as a measurement). **≡ Timeline**: every recorded moment, newest first, with day headings — the backlog's history rather than the folder's. **⌸ Sessions**: what a run actually got through, including how many of *its own* turns went on the backlog. **⚕ Gaps**: what the record does **not** know.

**Gaps is the view this needed most.** Every other view lists what the ledger knows, and a record that only ever shows what it knows looks complete. Coverage is stated as a share of the coded backlog; the codes with no history at all are named; the best-effort attributions are broken down by *reason* (`multi` · `none` · `stale` · `turn` · `guess`), each pressable straight into a filter; reconstructed rows are counted apart from observed ones; and closures with no session to point at are listed rather than quietly rendered as blanks.

**Two axes of filtering, and they mean different things on purpose.** *The item* — a query box over the code, the words, the closing note, **every agent and session id in its history and every event note**; area chips; state. *The record* — event kind, source, doubt, a date range, and three switches (only uncertain · only with a closing note · hide reconstructed). A row survives when it passes the item test **and still has a record left**, so a card with an empty body never appears. Nine orderings, `N of M` on screen whenever anything is narrowed, and every hover filled with the derived figures the row already implies.

**Three actions, and two of them ask twice.** **✎ Record** puts one event on the ledger by hand — a form on the page, stamped `panel`, which means real but belonging to no session, because a click happens in no conversation and a record that claimed one would point at a transcript where nothing happened. **⚕ Reconstruct** runs the backfill as a **preview**: it lists the exact rows it would write and the second press writes *those* — the same request with `apply` set, never a re-derivation, because a second scan can legitimately return different rows and a confirmation that applies something other than what it showed is worse than none. **↻ Rebuild the log** rewrites `Backloglog.md` from the ledger, keeping every line the human put in it. A row's right-click carries the same, plus *show only this*, *show it in the Backlog*, and four copies (code · closing stamp · every session id · the whole history as text).

**What it deliberately cannot do is edit the item.** No priority, no size, no queue — that is the Backlog tab's, and two surfaces writing the same field differently is a defect this system has already paid for once. The one line across is *Show it in the Backlog tab*. In the other direction, the backlog row's **"Where has this been worked?"** now lands **on the item** rather than merely on the tab, through the visible search box so the narrowing can be seen and removed.

**The bridge got the one op the panel added:** `projects.backloglog` rebuilds the document from the ledger, and the backfill body moved out of its op so a button and an agent cannot propose two different histories for one ledger. The Track payload is capped at 5,000 records per paint and **says so on the page** when it bites.

**Fixed on the way through: `AFHeader.wire` bound every `data-afact` on the page**, which is also what `afForm.js` puts on a card's Submit and Cancel — so pressing Save in *any* form card posted a spurious `{ type:'headerAction', action:'submit' }` alongside the page's own message. Harmless only for as long as no header action is ever named "submit" or "cancel". It is scoped to `.af-head` now.

## 3.95.0 — The parts come to the whole, a picker you can type into, and three pages whose bridge could only read (2026-08-09)

**‼️ ⚭ GATHER FAMILIES — THE PARTS COME TO THE WHOLE, AND THE WHOLE DOES NOT MOVE (U-24).** A family scattered through two hundred queue entries could only be collected by hand, one drag at a time, which is why nobody did it. The new button sits beside 🧹 *Tidy file order* and the pair is the point: **this one rearranges the QUEUE and moves no line; that one rearranges the FILE and moves no queue entry.** The **wholes keep their order among themselves** — where a family sits is a decision made about the whole, and a button that re-prioritised those could not be pressed twice without checking — and the parts come to it, keeping the order they already had, depth first. A part queued *ahead* of its whole comes back down to it, which is the case it is most often pressed for. One family at a time is on the row's own right-click menu (*Gather its parts under it*, drawn only where there is something to gather), `projects.backlog.gather` is the same write over the bridge, and a `[parent:: …]` chain that loops leaves every entry in the queue exactly once.

**And the answer to the question underneath it: dragging inside the ⊂ family view is deliberate, and it is the by-hand version of the same move.** A drop always lands against the WHOLE queue, so dropping a part on the lower half of its whole puts it directly behind it however many hidden entries were in between — including the one-parent-one-child case that looked impossible. It can therefore be a real move that looks like nothing happened, which is a legibility problem and not a correctness one: the drag handle's hover now says so while the list is narrowed.

**‼️ "— PART OF —" IS A BOX YOU TYPE INTO (U-21, U-22).** It was a native `<select>`, on the reasoning that typing a code jumps to it. That holds for a code you already know and fails for everything else: a `<select>` jumps on an option's **first characters only**, so nothing about an item except its code could be searched — and the question this control exists to answer is *"which of my open items is this a part of"*, which is asked with words far more often than with a code. It now filters live, by code **or** by words, in the extension's own query language (`AFQuery`, so `wildcard*`, `"phrase"` and `OR` work here exactly as in the search box); `↑ ↓` move, `Enter` takes the one that is lit, `✕` clears link and query together, and resting on a line opens the **whole item** beside it in the same panel the Next up strip uses. The row's own `↳` picker had the matching failure at the other end: last control on the row, so a menu anchored to it opened hard against the right edge of the panel and ran off it — and being anchored there it had to stay narrow, which is what forced fifty live items to be described in forty-six characters each. It opens **the width of the row** now, the same fix the Next up chips got, with room to say which item each line is.

**Being a part is drawn on the row, in every ordering (U-23).** The indent exists only in the family view; everywhere else the only sign was a badge at the far right of the line, which is not where the eye is when reading down a column of codes. The **code itself** says it now — italic, with a hairline down its leading edge in the same green the `⊂` badge uses; a link whose whole was deleted in Obsidian gets the same shape in the warning colour instead, since the row's `⚠` carries the why. Nothing else changes: fading the words would say "this matters less", which is the opposite of what being part of something means. The family indent went from 15px to 22px, because at fifteen a step was two characters wide and the eye had to measure one row's left edge against the next to see it at all.

**‼️ THREE PAGES COULD WRITE AND THEIR BRIDGE COULD ONLY READ (B-2).** The rule in `_REF/agentfarm-dev.md` is that a function a person can trigger reaches the bridge in the same commit; three surfaces had drifted from it. **Toolbox** could install a skill, a subagent, a command and an MCP server, copy one to another agent, remove one and switch a plugin or a server off — the bridge had `list`, `inspect` and `mcptools`. Now `toolbox.install` (with the `args`/`env`/`headers` no dialog chain could ever ask for, and an `edit` that keeps every key it was not asked about), `toolbox.copy` (`to:"user"` **shares** a skill, which moves it, so there is no shadow), `toolbox.remove` (`.afbak` beside whatever went) and `toolbox.enable` (the reversible half of remove). **One implementation, two doors:** the composition moved out of `toolboxPanel.ts` into `core/toolboxBridge.ts` and the page now calls it, so the two can never write different things. **Agents:** `agents.create`, `agents.roots`, `agents.pack.import` — the fleet card's own write, mission line included. **Studio had no bridge at all**, and it is the surface where that cost the most: producing media is exactly the long, many-step job an agent should drive. Eight ops — `studio.productions` · `studio.assets` · `studio.production.new` · `studio.scene.status` · `studio.assign` · `studio.triage` · `studio.rerun` (plain · vary · feedback, each a different question) · `studio.export`.

**Two smaller things.** The **Open Capability Matrix** icon is `$(compass)` instead of `$(table)` — round where the sidebar row wanted round (U-20). And `moveSkillScope` takes a `home` override like every other reader in `core/toolbox.ts`; without it, a test of "share this skill" could only be written by writing into the machine's own `~/.claude`.

## 3.94.0 — A bug hunt over five releases: a page nothing could read, an assignment that never landed, and a store the bridge had never heard of (2026-08-09)

**‼️ TWO SOURCE FILES WERE INVISIBLE TO EVERY SEARCH IN THIS REPO (X-12).** `media/studio.js` and `src/core/portablePaths.ts` each carried a **literal NUL byte** — a deliberate separator, written as the byte rather than as the escape. ripgrep calls a file with a NUL in it *binary* and reports `binary file matches` with no lines, so `grep` over this repo — by a person or by an agent — silently skipped the entire Studio page and the whole portable-path engine. Not a stylistic point: it is how a file stops being read. `portablePaths.ts` now writes the same character as a six-character escape, which the runtime reads identically and every other tool reads as an ordinary line; the Studio's separator became a printable one for the reason below. A test refuses a raw NUL anywhere under `media/`, `src/` or `test/`.

**‼️ AND BEHIND ONE OF THEM, A FEATURE THAT NEVER WORKED.** Finding it needed the file to be searchable first. The Studio's assign picker builds a target as `<productionId><separator><sceneId>` in `media/studio.js` — and `studioPanel.ts` split that string on a **SPACE**. So assigning a media output to a scene filed it under a project id that was really the whole joined string, with no scene at all, silently, because every id involved is machine-generated and nothing downstream could tell the difference. A space was never a safe separator either: production and scene ids come out of a JSON file the user edits by hand. There is one exported `SCENE_TARGET_SEP` now, the panel splits at the **first** one (a production alone carries none; detach is the empty string), and a test pins the two spellings together.

**‼️ MEMORY WAS NOT ON THE BRIDGE AT ALL — not even a read.** The memory page learned to make a file in 3.91.0 and the bridge was never told, which is the rule in `_REF/agentfarm-dev.md` being broken by the very release that wrote it down. It matters more here than almost anywhere: an agent's memory is the one store the agent itself is expected to keep, and until now it could only do so by writing files behind the extension's back — no index line, no refresh, no frontmatter convention. **`memory.list`** answers the honest question about a memory file, which is not "does it exist" but `inIndex` — will the agent ever open it, the index being the only thing loaded every session. **`memory.add`** calls the very writer the page calls, so the file and its `MEMORY.md` line are written together, and it refuses a name that cannot be a file name, a missing description (that line *is* the index entry) and a type outside the convention.

**Living checks were readable and not writable.** 3.92.0 made them rows a person can add and remove on the page and left the bridge with the read op it already had. **`living.check.add`** and **`living.check.remove`** close it — keyed **by label**, so calling add twice tunes a check instead of growing a pile of near-identical ones nobody can tell apart, and remove says so when the label is not there rather than reporting a no-op as success.

**A new structural test: every message a page posts has somebody listening.** A webview and its panel talk over one untyped channel, and nothing in TypeScript connects `postMessage({type:'x'})` to `case 'x':` — a renamed handler is a dead control that compiles and ships. `test_page_messages.js` checks eleven page/panel pairs in **one direction only**, on purpose: "a `case` nobody sends" cannot be decided by reading (pages post through shared helpers, dispatch tables and computed names), and a test that guesses there would cry wolf and be switched off within a week.

**Blueprint, on both sides.** The shared form component — `media/afForm.js`, the architectural change 3.91.0 turned on and the reason eight surfaces stopped being second-class — **was not in the Blueprint at all**; it has its own section next to the filters contract it is a sibling of, in both languages, with the rule for the next surface: whatever object the list shows, the card that makes it is on the same page. The memory ops, the live permission vocabulary and the living-check write path are documented where they belong.

## 3.93.0 — Why a session with every permission open suddenly started asking, and the two-button dialog that had no way out (2026-08-09)

**‼️ THE ANSWER FIRST, BECAUSE IT IS NOT OURS: THE CLI CHANGED THE RULE ABOUT WHO MAY INHERIT `bypassPermissions` (S-18).** `~/.claude/settings.json` has said `permissions.defaultMode: "bypassPermissions"` for months, and hosted sessions never asked for anything. The build installed on 2026-08-08 carries a gate the one before it does not — the strings are in the binary and only in the new one:

> `settings defaultMode "bypassPermissions" ignored for a VS Code-owned session without the allow-bypass setting`
> `Permission mode bypassPermissions from settings was ignored — enable the "Claude Code: Allow Dangerously Skip Permissions" setting in VS Code to consent to it`

with the telemetry key `tengu_settings_bypass_unconsented_noninteractive_ignored`, **present in `claude.exe`, absent from `claude.exe.old`**. A hosted Console session is exactly that: VS Code-owned and non-interactive. Two older kill-switches sit beside it — `bypassPermissions mode is disabled by feature gate` (`tengu_disable_bypass_permissions_mode`) and `…disabled by settings` — and they are consulted per call, not once at startup, which is why the asking can begin **in the middle of a turn** rather than at a session boundary. Nothing in Agent Farm broke and no setting was touched: the CLI stopped honouring a file it used to honour.

**‼️ WHAT WAS OURS: THE WAY OUT WAS A DEAD CONTROL, AND IT FAILED IN SILENCE.** Told to switch, the CLI answers plainly — measured against 2.1.226:

> `error: Cannot set permission mode to bypassPermissions because the session was not launched with --dangerously-skip-permissions`

`setPermissionMode` was the one control request sent fire-and-forget: written to stdin, reported as done, never reconciled. The reducer only ever acted on a *success* carrying a mode, so the refusal changed nothing, said nothing, and the picker snapped back on the next repaint — which is precisely "I moved it to bypass and it kept asking". Worse, the **"grant + retry"** button sent the agent *"Permission granted — please retry the action you were refused"* on the strength of a successful **write**, so it retried a permission nobody had. The switch is now a tracked request like `setModel` and `setEffort`: a refusal is a line in the conversation in the CLI's own words, an acceptance shows **what the CLI accepted** rather than what was asked for, and the retry nudge goes out only after the yes. Hosted sessions carry `--allow-dangerously-skip-permissions`, which — the CLI's own help — *"enables bypassing all permission checks as an option, without it being enabled by default"*: it grants nothing, it makes the panel's own ⚠ control possible.

**‼️ AND THE MISSING THIRD BUTTON.** A terminal has always offered three ways out of a permission prompt: yes, **yes and stop asking me about this kind of command**, no. The panel had the outer two, so a run that needed `npm` twenty times asked twenty times and the only escape was widening the entire session. The middle one is a real capability of the channel: the allow answer may carry `updatedPermissions`, and the CLI installs them before it runs the tool — measured, three `touch` commands in `default` mode went from **three asks to one**. Bash is scoped by **command prefix** (`npm:*`), the way the CLI writes its own bash rules, because "allow every Bash" is not what a person means when they approve `npm run package`; every other tool is scoped by name. The button says which rule it installs, the rule is `destination: 'session'` and is never written to any settings file, and it is not offered on an AskUserQuestion, which is not a permission at all.

**Three rows in the live permissions picker could never have worked.** It was fed the *launcher's* list, and a running session speaks a shorter vocabulary. Put to the CLI one at a time: `auto` → `error "auto mode unavailable for this model"`, `manual` → success but the CLI answers back `mode: "default"`, and the launcher's own "Default" row (the empty string, meaning *pass no flag*) → `error "must be one of acceptEdits, auto, bypassPermissions, default, dontAsk, plan"`. The live picker is its own list now — `default · acceptEdits · plan · dontAsk · ⚠ bypassPermissions` — the start picker is unchanged, `auto` is gone from the refusal box's grant buttons for the same measured reason, and `console.permissionMode` validates against the live vocabulary and reports the request as **sent**, because the CLI has the last word and it comes back on the wire.

## 3.92.1 — Two bridge arguments that documented a default they never had (2026-08-09)

**‼️ `projects.backlog.check` REFUSED EVERY CALL THAT LEFT `state` OUT** — which is every call that just closes an item, the commonest thing the op does. Its contract says "default done"; the code said `optStr(args, 'state') ?? 'done'`, and `optStr` returns `''` for an absent argument, so `??` never fired and the guard rejected the empty string with a message about a value the caller had not sent: *"state must be one of open, doing, done, cancelled"*. Found the only way this kind of thing is found — by using it. `||` is the operator that reads an absent string. The same slip made the documented `destCode` alias on `projects.backlog.move` silently dead: `optStr('dest') ?? optStr('destCode')` can never reach the second one.

## 3.92.0 — The sweep finishes: living checks, the vault, the studio, an agent itself — and evals get the page they never had (2026-08-09)

**‼️ FIVE MORE SURFACES, AND ONE THAT HAD TO BE BUILT (U-13 / U-14).** 3.91.0 gave duties, the toolbox and memory a form; this finishes the list. **Living checks** were a quickpick that led to five more boxes and never once showed you the list it was adding to — which is exactly why "edit the JSON" had to be offered as its third option; the checks are rows on the page they become rows on, one open at a time, and the whole list is written on Save so a reorder is one write and Cancel is not a lie. **The vault** had four buttons that each posted to the host for one more box, so setting a secret's name, its value, what it is for and who may use it was four separate trips; it is one card, and the value still only ever travels one way — nothing sends a stored value back to the page, and an empty value box on an edit means "keep what is in the keychain". **The studio** had five: a production (a title, then its scenes as ONE COMMA-SEPARATED LINE, so a scene called "Opening, wide" became two scenes silently — it is one per line now), a variation asking for a whole JSON input in a single-line box, a critique, an assignment flattened into an indented quickpick, and an export asking which production → which frame rate → how long, three in a row.

**‼️ AN AGENT — THE OBJECT AT THE CENTRE OF THIS EXTENSION — COULD ONLY BE MADE BY A DIALOG CHAIN.** `newAgent` asked which root folder (a quickpick, skipped when there is only one, so on most machines it was invisible) and then a name. It could say nothing about the two things that decide what the agent IS from its very first session: **its default model**, and **the line of CLAUDE.md that says what it is for**. Both are on the fleet page's card now, and the mission line is written straight into the template instead of leaving "Describe this agent's identity, mission and boundaries here" for somebody to come back to — which is the state most abandoned agents are in. Importing a pack lands on the same card: the OS file picker stays, and what followed it (a root quickpick, a name box, and a modal that recited the pack's contents as text) is now the card stating those contents and refusing a clashing name in the form.

**‼️ AND EVALS FINALLY EXIST AS A PLACE.** Every other object in the sweep had a page that listed it and could not make it. Evals had **neither** — four palette commands were the whole feature. So a regression suite you spent an afternoon building could only be read back one quickpick line at a time, `syncMemory`-style, and the question the suite exists to answer — *which cases fail now that passed last week?* — had no answer short of re-running everything and reading a generated markdown file. There is a page: the cases, the runs, and **the case-by-case verdict of the latest run against the one before it** — ⛔ regressed, ✅ fixed, 🆕 new. A case is normally born from a bad trace, so the card opens with the low-scored sessions and failed verifications and fills the input from the one you pick, recording where it came from so the same trace is never turned into two cases. The A/B card offers this agent's open **suggestions** as variant B, because a suggestion is already a one-line rule somebody proposed for exactly this agent, and it defaults the comparison prompts to the eval cases — a suite you already trust is the fairest comparison there is. On the bridge: `evals.list`, `evals.add`, `evals.runs` (with the same regressed/fixed verdict) and `living.checks`. **Running** a set is deliberately NOT on the bridge: it spends one agent call and one judge call per case, and that decision stays with the person looking at the modal.

**Two capture boxes instead of two input boxes.** `queueAdd` and `backlogAdd` asked for a line of text in a box that can state the text and nothing else. They open the page's own capture box now — the queue's carries the target and the urgency; the backlog's carries the area, the type, the priority, the effort, the queue position and the parent — and both show you the list you are adding to while you type.

**‼️ AND A BUG THIS RELEASE FOUND IN THE LAST ONE.** 3.91.0 added six right-click items (edit/copy/pause/delete on a duty, copy-to-another-agent and distil on a memory file) to the catalogue in `core/ctxmenu.ts` — and they never reached `package.json`, so they did not appear. The test that guards exactly this drift reads the **compiled** catalogue, and running the suite without compiling first compares last build's catalogue against today's manifest and passes, having measured nothing. `test/run-all.js` compiles first now and fails loudly if it cannot; the nine items (those six plus the three on an eval case) are in the manifest.

**Measured, honestly this time.** The first sweep's numbers over-counted: cutting a command body only at the next registration makes the LAST command of a `context.subscriptions.push(…)` block swallow everything up to the next block, which is how a three-line `missionStepOutput` was reported as a fifteen-box monster. With the closing-line rule: of **219** commands, **28** use an input box or a quick pick and **2** are chains of two or more — from 58 and 20. The remaining twenty-eight are listed **by name** in `test/test_dialog_sweep.js` with the reason each one stays: a quickpick over rows that already exist is what a quickpick is for, and a single box asking a single string about a thing that already exists is not a form. Converting them would be a decision, not a completion.

## 3.91.0 — The pages that listed an object can finally make one: duties, the toolbox, and memory (2026-08-09)

**‼️ THE FORM IS A COMPONENT NOW, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE STORY (U-13 / U-14).** The Tasks page got a real form in 3.80.0 and finished the job in 3.83.0 — and then kept it. Eleven hundred lines of composer that only a task, a schedule, a trigger, a template and a workflow could reach; every other surface stayed on chains of VS Code boxes, and the measurement says why that keeps happening rather than being a matter of will: writing a form was a day's work per page, so nobody did it, and the platform's own boxes won by default. So the card is `media/afForm.js` and the CSS is in `common.css`, both shipped to every page from `htmlShell` — a component a page has to opt into is a component the next surface skips, which is exactly how **eight** surfaces ended up listing an object they could not make. A field declares the key it writes and the type it writes it as, so `read()` hands back a typed draft and nothing lists its field ids twice; `{redraw:true}` marks the fields that change what *exists*; the OS file picker stays, as one button next to the box it fills, because a folder chooser is the one thing a webview genuinely cannot draw and typing `D:\zone\know\note\…` by hand is what the dialog chains were defending.

**‼️ A DUTY IS MADE ON THE AGENT'S OWN PAGE.** That page has drawn an agent's standing duties for longer than most of this extension has existed, and it could not make one. `addDuty` asked five questions in a row — title, then cadence, then *how you would like to name the watch target*, then the target, then what to do about it — `editDuty` asked five more and started from the top every time, `addDutyFromTemplate` wrote a duty you had never seen and told you to right-click it to fix, and `copyDuty` was a quickpick of agent names. Esc anywhere in any of them threw the lot away. One card now, under the table it belongs to, and every route opens it: the button, a row's **edit**, the palette, the sidebar tree, the right-click menu. The templates became the card's **Start from** picker, so a proven pattern lands in fields you can still change instead of straight into the file. And the row grew the rest of itself — **edit · copy · pause · ↑ ↓ · ✕**, plus the same four in its menu, because a page that shows six facts about a duty and offers no way to act on any of them was sending you to the sidebar tree to pause something.

**‼️ THE TOOLBOX MAKES THE TOOL — INCLUDING THE THREE FIELDS NOTHING COULD EVER ASK FOR.** Four routes, four vocabularies: `+ new skill` posted to the host, which asked two input boxes; `addMcpServer` asked four in the palette; `editMcpServer` flattened a server to **one line of text** and kept or dropped whatever else its definition held by luck; `copyMcpServer` and `duplicateSkill` were a quickpick of agent names each. None of them could state an MCP server's **`args`**, its **`env`** or its **`headers`** — which is precisely what decides whether the server it just wrote can authenticate. The card asks for all three: arguments **one per line**, because a path with a space in it is one argument and a single text box can never round-trip that, and environment and headers as `KEY=value` lines with `{secret:NAME}` substituted from the vault at launch. Transport is a field near the top rather than the first question, so changing your mind about stdio-versus-http redraws half the card instead of starting over. An edit now carries over every key it was not asked about **explicitly** rather than by accident.

**‼️ AND MEMORY — SIX COMMANDS, ONE PAGE.** The memory graph drew every file an agent has and could not create, copy, index, distil, compare or query a single one. `syncMemory` was **five quickpicks stacked** (which file → which action → left copy → right copy → confirm); `fixMemoryIndex` was a multi-pick with the index it was repairing nowhere in sight; `askMemory` asked in a box and then answered in a **quickpick of one-line labels**, which is the worst possible container for a paragraph of recalled text. Every one is a card beside the graph now. The repair card draws both kinds of problem — a file with no pointer, a pointer with no file — as rows with their own ticks, and sends back the exact lines still ticked, so a plan recomputed between the drawing and the press cannot apply something you never saw. The shared-memory card shows the whole picture at once: which names exist on two agents, which have drifted apart, every copy with its age and size, compare-two, make-a-merge-draft and choose-the-source-of-truth. The fleet query draws its hits with score, agent and enough of the passage to recognise. The buttons above the graph count what is waiting — `Repair index (2)`, `Shared (1⚠)` — because "is there anything to fix here?" used to be a question the command could only answer by being run.

**What deliberately did not move.** The **modal** in front of anything destructive (deleting a duty, overwriting N copies of a shared file) — a page-side warning is missable while the focus is elsewhere. The **OS file picker**. And **`vscode.diff`**: a real diff editor is a better reader than anything a webview could draw, so comparing two copies and reviewing a distilled draft still open one.

**Measured, both times the same way** — every `registerCommand` body in `extension.ts`, cut at the next registration: **58 → 42** commands using an input box or a quick pick, **20 → 13** chains of two or more. The remaining thirteen are the next surfaces (evals, the studio, agent creation, living checks, the vault). One thing the number does not see, and now says so in `docs/CONVENTIONS.md`: **the scan only reads `extension.ts`**, and the Toolbox's own `+ new skill` was two input boxes hiding inside `toolboxPanel.ts` where no measurement ever looked.

## 3.90.0 — An item can be part of another one, and this time the queue was designed for it (2026-08-09)

**‼️ M-2 — A BACKLOG ITEM MAY NOW BE PART OF ANOTHER, AND THE LINK IS A FIELD.** `[parent:: P-4]`, on the child's own line, beside `[effort:: S]` and `[plan:: …]`. There is a **part of** box in the Capture panel and a `↳` on every row's hover actions; the row then wears **↳ P-4** (press it to go to the whole) and the whole wears **⊂ 2/3** (press it to see only that family — open of total, because that is the number an epic is read for).

**This is a rebuild of something that was removed, and the design is the removal's post-mortem.** The backlog carried a hierarchy from its first version, drawn from the file's own **indentation** and shown inside a list ordered by the **queue** — two unrelated structures on one screen. The first time anybody used it a child was drawn under an item that was not its parent and moved somewhere else on every re-sort; it was taken out whole in 3.85.0 with one note for whoever rebuilt it: *the design has to cover the queue, because drawing a tree in a flat queue does not work.* So three rules, each one a fix for a way it went wrong:

- **The link is DATA, not layout.** No line moves because of it, ever. 🧹 Tidy still sorts each area by code, 🗄 Archive still moves one closed line, and indentation in your file means exactly what it meant before: nothing. The link rides *in* the line, so it survives all three — and being edited in Obsidian.
- **The CODE stays flat.** A part gets the next free number **in its own area**, so a `U` item being part of a `P` epic is the ordinary case rather than the impossible one. A dotted `P-4.1` would have forbidden exactly that. A code says which item, never where in a tree.
- **The QUEUE does not know.** A part and its whole are queued **separately** and either can be worked first. Nothing is a "container", nothing is dropped from `next`, nothing is dragged along by anything else. The relation is a **mark**, never a position — and the one place a tree is *drawn* is the new **family** ordering, which nests each whole above its parts (families in the order their heads are queued in) and is a way of *looking*, not an order anything is worked in. Even there, a part whose whole is filtered off the screen is drawn at the margin rather than under a stranger.

**It is composition, not dependency, and the two are never derived from each other.** `⛔` is what an item *waits on*; a part does not wait on its whole. **Closing a whole does not close its parts** — it says which are still open, and they stay in the queue. Archiving a closed whole leaves its open parts where they are and says so, because the row you were reading them under has just moved.

**Refused where a mistake would be silent, reported where the file is already the user's.** A link naming something that is not there, or itself, or one of its own descendants (a loop) is refused **before a byte is written** — and the row picker never even offers those, because a control that offers what the write will reject is a control that lies. A file that already contains a broken link is **reported, never corrected**: the `↳` goes amber, the field is left exactly as typed, and filing that capture still works — a link the user broke in Obsidian must not make an unrelated action impossible.

**Filtering and asking for it.** The ⚙ panel gained a **family** axis (part of something · has parts · stands on its own — a middle-of-chain item is honestly both), and `⊂` sets a one-code family filter that says so in a removable chip. On the bridge: `projects.backlog` and `projects.backlog.next` both take `parent` (its direct parts) and `parent` + `deep` (the whole family, head included — what "do P-4" means), `parent:"none"` asks the other half (what stands on its own), and `.add` / `.set` write or clear the link. Every item everywhere now carries `parent` and `children`, and a child in the session brief carries `↳P-4` — an agent handed "add the retry form" without "part of U-9" does the item and misses the point of it.

## 3.89.0 — The backlog is one press from the tree, a hover stops opening off the edge of the window, and a setting nobody declares stops sitting in your file (2026-08-09)

**‼️ THE BACKLOG HAS ITS OWN BUTTON, AND IT FOLLOWS THE STATUS BAR (P-11).** The page you are in most took three moves to reach: open Projects, find the project, find the tab. There is an icon on the **AGENTS** strip now — between the capability matrix and `+`, at the end of the reading row — and it opens the project page **already on Backlog**. It does not ask which project: the window has an active project in the status bar, and a shortcut that made you say the same thing twice is exactly what U-16 fixed for sessions. It asks only when there is nothing active, with the **one** picker every other surface uses — and the answer becomes the window's active project, so it is asked once rather than at every press. A project deleted while it was the active one counts as no answer, not as an error. Underneath, the panel gained a **one-shot** "open on this tab": the tab it remembers is echoed back on every refresh (the host builds only the open tab's data), so a shortcut that set *that* would drag the reader back to Backlog every time anything repainted.

**‼️ A QUEUE CHIP'S HOVER OPENED OFF THE RIGHT OF THE WINDOW (U-19).** The panel behind a **Next up** chip is up to 760px wide and it opened at the chip's own left edge — so for every chip in the right-hand half of the strip the item was there and unreadable, most of it past the edge of the screen. The row's `more` has always been right about this, because a backlog row is a single positioning context and CSS can hand the box the whole width of the line. A chip cannot: its distance from the strip's left edge depends on where the flex wrap fell, and that is only knowable after layout. So the two numbers — how far the chip sits from the strip's left edge, and how wide the strip is — are **measured on the way in**, once per chip, and the box opens at the strip's left edge capped by the strip's width. Fixed horizontal place, whichever chip you are on, exactly like `more`. The chip stays the **vertical** anchor on purpose: the box opens directly under it with no gap, so the pointer can still travel into it and scroll — hanging the whole thing off the strip would have opened it below the *last* wrapped line, out of reach across the chips in between, which is the same panel with its scrolling taken away.

**‼️ AND THE DEAD `agentFarm.projects` SETTING IS GONE FROM YOUR SETTINGS FILE (P-12).** 3.58.0 stopped declaring it; that does not delete what somebody typed. So VS Code has been marking the block "Unknown Configuration Setting", and — the part that mattered — it was still there to be read by any older VSIX installed to check a symptom, which is the trap 3.88.0 answered from the *other* side with a tombstone. This answers it at the source: on first run the value is **archived** to `retired-settings.json` beside the store, and only then is the key removed — from every scope it could have been written in. The archive is written **before** the removal, never after: a crash between the two costs a copy too many, and the other order loses the value outright. That is what makes doing it without asking honest — nothing here can tell a value migrated years ago from one on a machine that never ran the importer, so it does not try to; it moves rather than deletes, says so once, and puts the copy one press away. One-shot by a marker rather than by "is it still there", because a user who types the key back has said something, and housekeeping that argues with them every window is a bug.

## 3.88.0 — One row, one mark; rolling a version back stops leaving a mark; and a name taken from a state, measured across every transcript (2026-08-09)

**‼️ A STEP ROW CARRIED TWO ICONS FOR THE SAME FACT, AND THE FIXED ONE SAID THE LESS (U-8).** Two glyph vocabularies met on one line. The old one is a ROLE picture — 🔧 for a tool call, 📄 for a result — drawn in the row's left gutter; the new one is the step KIND (`⌘` local · `⇄` MCP · `⌗` web · `⑂` sub-agent · `⚙` harness · `↩` result), which was glued to the front of the text. So every tool row in the Console's live stream and every tool row in the transcript drew both, always as a pair, always about the same step. And the survivor is not a coin toss: **🔧 is drawn identically on a web fetch, an MCP call and a Read off this disk**, while the chip is the whole point — it says where the cost came from. In the Console the kind chip **moved into the gutter**, where the eye already scans, and nothing sits in front of the words any more; the gutter is sized for a 10px uppercase word, so the glyph is given back its own size and its contrast. In the transcript the chip was already in the right place (it leads the meta line), so only the picture beside it came off. Two things deliberately untouched: the **filter chips** keep 🔧 — a control that names a category is not a mark on a row, and it is how tool traffic is picked on four pages — and the **dialog rows** keep 🧑 and 🤖, which name a SPEAKER, something no step kind does. An error result keeps `⚠`: `↩ tool result` is true of every result row and says nothing about that one.

**‼️ ROLLING A VERSION BACK WAS LEAVING A PERMANENT MARK (X-8).** Projects were once declared in an `agentFarm.projects` **setting**, and a one-time import moved them into `projects.json`, writing `migrated: true` there so it could never run twice. 3.58.0 deleted the old model: the setting, the importer, and — the part that bites — **the marker**. Dropping a contribution point does not delete what somebody typed, so the setting is still sitting in `settings.json`; the moment an older VSIX is installed to check a symptom, that build finds a store with no marker, re-runs the import it still carries, and writes a project nobody asked for back into the registry. Measured on this machine: a folderless `Me.DEV.AgentFarm` claiming developer/basit/manager, created 13 seconds after a 3.52.0 install. **A version rollback is a diagnostic, and a diagnostic must not leave a mark**, so the tombstone is written again — and a store left without one is stamped **on load**, not on the next project edit, because otherwise the trap stays armed for however long you do not touch a project. Nothing in this version reads it; that is exactly the point, and a test holds it, because "unused" is the argument that would delete it and re-arm the trap. A machine with no store is not given one just for being read (there, an old build importing the setting is the migration doing its job), and an unreadable store is left byte-identical rather than written over — the link table is the one thing here that cannot be rebuilt.

**A session named after a state: verified across every transcript, not argued about (S-13).** The reported session (`286f8533`) is named from its `/goal` brief now, and the rule behind it was checked the only way worth checking — against the whole vault. All 343 transcripts were walked with the index's own `conversationTitle`: **320 produced a name, and none of them is machinery** — not a bracketed notice, not a command block, not a slash command, not a shell passthrough, not a harness announcement. The two rules that get it right are the ones added in 3.85.0 and 3.86.0: only a message that is **entirely** a bracketed notice is plumbing (`[Image #2] look at this` is a person talking), and a command's **argument** names the session when it reads as prose.

**And the inventory that X-2 was waiting on.** Every view/selection control in the extension, page by page, with what it defaults to and what happens to it today — written to the project's notes for you to mark up. The finding that shapes the whole item: **what is "remembered" today is remembered only until the tab closes.** Pages write their choices to the webview's own `setState`, and no `WebviewPanelSerializer` is registered anywhere in the codebase, so nothing carries that state into the next window. Which means "should this be remembered?" and "for how long?" are one question, not two.

## 3.87.0 — Items that were never lost, only filed under the wrong heading; every clock says which UTC it is; and a tab narrows by its own columns (2026-08-08)

**‼️ SIX BACKLOG ITEMS WERE MISSING FROM `Backlog.md`, AND THEY WERE NEVER MISSING (P-10).** `A-4` and `P-6`…`P-10` were sitting under `## U — Arayüz`. Nothing had been archived, nothing had been dropped, and there is no second store: the panel reads the one file the project declares with `af-role: backlog`, and it draws every row correctly — because it groups by the **CODE**, not by the heading the line happens to sit under. So the extension showed a healthy P section while the file did not have one, and the only reader who could see the damage was the person scrolling their own document to `## P` and finding five items gone. **Measured**, and this is the part worth keeping: `projects.backlog.add` places correctly — replayed against the file as it stood before the drift, all eight of that day's new items land under their own headings. What put them there wrote the lines **directly** rather than through the op. Then `🧹 Tidy file order` cemented it: it sorts **runs of consecutive items** and by design cannot cross a heading, so it neatly ordered `A-4 · P-6 … P-10 · U-1 · U-3` and made a mistake look deliberate. **Tidy now has the other half.** It first carries a coded item back under its own area's heading, then sorts — reporting the codes that moved **by name**, because "3 items reordered" is housekeeping and "P-6, P-7 were under the wrong heading" is the thing worth reading. An area with no heading in the file is never invented, the archive is left alone, and the queue is untouched by either move: it names codes, and a code does not care which line it is on. The real file is repaired: 71 items in, 71 items out, queue byte-identical.

**‼️ THE TAGS ARE `#byAI` AND `#byREQ` (K-8).** Your spelling, your capitals. And the capitals are now safe from the thing that would have eaten them silently: every tag in this system is **matched folded** — everything downstream compares in lower case — and a rebuilt line writes back whatever the parser returned. So the first time anybody clicked a priority badge, `#byAI` would have become `#byai` in your vault, with nothing to say it had happened. These two are folded on the way in and restored on the way out, so `#byai`, `#BYAI` and `#ByAi` typed by hand in Obsidian all land on the one spelling, and a rebuild cannot change it. In plain words: **`#byAI`** = an agent noticed this while working and filed it itself — a *proposal*, not something you agreed to. **`#byREQ`** = an agent typed it because you asked. A line you wrote yourself carries **neither**.

**‼️ EVERY CLOCK IN THE EXTENSION SAYS WHICH UTC IT IS (U-10).** Everything here is a record of when a machine did something, and machines state their times in UTC — a transcript, a plan-quota window, a rate limit, a scheduled run. The pages rendered them in whatever zone this computer is set to, so `14:32` was a fact plus an offset nothing on screen mentioned, and checking a run against a provider's own log meant doing the arithmetic in your head. There is **one clock** now (`media/afTime.js`), loaded by the pages *and* by the extension host, so the two sides cannot disagree: **every** time carries UTC in its **hover** — three lines, the moment to the second, its UTC, and this machine's offset *at that moment*, not today's — and a time with **room around it** carries UTC inline: `20:14 (17:14 UTC)`. That means page footers, card sub-lines, session and turn stamps, status-bar and sidebar hovers, and **exported files**, which have no hover to hide the offset in. Table cells stay narrow on purpose: widening every date column by eleven characters to repeat what the hover already says would cost the columns carrying the actual measurements. A missing timestamp still prints **nothing** — never `Invalid Date`, never 1 Jan 1970.

**‼️ EVERY TOOLBOX TAB NARROWS BY ITS OWN COLUMNS (S-15).** Several hundred rows and one text box, which answers *"does this word appear"* — the wrong question nearly every time: **"the Anthropic ones that are actually enabled"** is two axes, and typing `anthropic` returns everything that merely mentions them. Each tab now carries the facets its own table already shows: Skills *scope · used/never used* · Subagents *scope · model* · Commands *scope · namespace* · MCP *scope · transport · enabled/disabled · probed/failed/not probed* · Hooks *scope · event* · Plugins *enabled/disabled · author · marketplace · category* — with a sort (name, most used, enabled first, largest, scope) and a live count of what survived. Facets are **ANDed**, and their options are read off the **data**, so a new marketplace, transport or hook event appears in the picker on its own instead of going stale. The selection is kept **per tab** — building a view is four clicks and stepping into MCP for a glance should not throw it away — while the text box stays per page, since typed words rarely mean the same thing on the next tab. `✕ clear` drops both, for that tab only. **And the same questions are on the bridge:** `toolbox.list` takes `q` · `scope` · `agent` · `kind` · `state` · `event` · `transport` · `author` · `marketplace` · `model`, ANDed, so what the page can ask an agent can ask.

**‼️ THE BACKLOG CAPTURE BOX KEEPS THE HEIGHT YOU DRAGGED IT TO (U-18).** The console composer learned this in 3.86.0; the capture box had the same bug and it looked stranger, because only *some* gestures throw the height away. Typing does not repaint, so nothing happens while you write — but picking an **area** or a **queue position** does, and the box snapped back to a slit mid-item, while the controls beside it were being filled in. Switching tabs and coming back did it too. The dragged height is kept beside the draft text, for the same reason the draft is kept at all, and it survives every repaint, a host push and a tab. A window resize is not mistaken for a drag. The ↺ that resets the five axes does **not** shrink the box: it resets choices, not the writing surface — the same promise its hover already makes about your words.

**A decision, and it is that nothing changes (X-9).** The İ bug was real and was fixed in 3.86.0 (`İş türü` filed itself as `i-ş-türü`). The question left over was whether decision **file names** should fold to ASCII as well.
